\boxed{S_{6}=\Bigl\{W_{6}:{\nabla}_{\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}=0,\hskip 9.24994pt{\Gamma}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}={\Gamma}_{\nu\mu}^{\lambda}\Bigr\}}

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View):

\underbrace{\{\ A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\ A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\ A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2},A_{3}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\ W_{3}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{3}}\;\longrightarrow\;

\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\ W_{3},A_{3}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{4}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\ W_{5}\}}_{S_{5}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\ W_{6}\}}_{S_{5}}

Appendix J
From Classical Thermodynamics to Statistical Mechanics

J.1
Classical Thermodynamics

Classical thermodynamics is the macroscopic theory dealing with heat, work, temperature, and their relations to energy, radiation, and the physical properties of matter. It establishes fundamental principles (often referred to as laws) that govern the exchanges of energy and the directionality of processes in macroscopic systems.

J.1.1
Axioms

1.

Zeroth Law of Thermodynamics

\forall\,\text{systems }A,B,C:\bigl(A\text{ in thermal equilibrium with }C\bigr)\wedge\bigl(B\text{ in thermal equilibrium with }C\bigr)

\;\Longrightarrow\;\bigl(A\text{ in thermal equilibrium with }B\bigr).

This establishes the concept of temperature as a fundamental property and justifies consistent temperature measurement.

2.

First Law of Thermodynamics

\mathrm{d}U\;=\;\delta Q\;-\;\delta W.

There exists an extensive state function
U
called the internal energy. Its change in any process is equal to the heat supplied to the system minus the work done by the system.

3.

Second Law of Thermodynamics

\Delta S\;=\;\int\frac{\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}}{T}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{(for a reversible process)},

and for an isolated system,

\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\;\geq\;0.

Entropy
S
is thus a state function, given by the integral of the reversible heat exchange divided by the temperature. In any spontaneous (irreversible) process, the total entropy of an isolated system does not decrease.

4.

Third Law of Thermodynamics

\lim_{T\to 0}S\;=\;S_{0},

where
S_{0}
is a universal constant (often taken as zero for a perfectly ordered system). As temperature approaches absolute zero, the entropy of a perfect crystal approaches a constant minimum value.

J.1.2
Completeness

1.

Thermal Equilibrium.
(
Axiom 1
) underpins the concept of temperature, enabling consistent thermometric definitions and comparisons.

2.

Energy Conservation.
(
Axiom 2
) ensures that all forms of energy transfer (heat, work) can be accounted for through the internal energy of the system.

3.

Definition and Directionality via Entropy.
(
Axiom 3
) explains how entropy is defined (
\Delta S=\int\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}/T
in reversible processes) and clarifies that in real (irreversible) processes, the entropy of an isolated system can never decrease.

4.

Behavior at Absolute Zero.
(
Axiom 4
) characterizes the limiting behavior of systems at the lowest temperatures, completing the description of equilibrium thermodynamics.

From these four, one can analyze heat engines, refrigerators, phase transitions, and a wide range of thermodynamic processes, describing energy exchanges, maximum efficiency of cycles, and temperature limits.

J.1.3
Independence

1.

Zeroth Law.
cannot be deduced from energy conservation or entropy statements; it establishes the transitive property of thermal equilibrium and the concept of temperature.

2.

First Law.
is not derivable from the existence of thermal equilibrium or entropy principles alone; it adds a distinct statement about energy being a state function and conserved in processes.

3.

Second Law.
introduces the definition of entropy (through
\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}/T
) and its non-decreasing property in isolated systems; this does not follow from thermal equilibrium (zeroth law) or energy conservation (first law).

4.

Third Law.
focuses on the entropy’s limiting value at zero temperature. It cannot be derived from the other axioms, which govern equilibrium, energy conservation, and irreversibility but do not dictate the absolute value of entropy as
T\to 0
.

J.2
Statistical Mechanics

Statistical mechanics provides a framework to describe a system of many constituents (e.g., particles) in terms of probability distributions over its microscopic configurations. It connects microscopic states and their probabilistic weights to macroscopic observables. Below is a list of axioms that includes the familiar foundations (state space, probabilistic description, equilibrium stationarity, ergodicity), a statistical formula for entropy, and the statistical analogs of the thermodynamic laws.

J.2.1
Axioms

1.

State Space (Phase Space) Postulate

\text{A system with }f\text{ degrees of freedom is represented by a point in a }

2f\text{-dimensional phase space}.

Each microscopic configuration (microstate) of the system is specified by generalized coordinates and momenta.

2.

Probabilistic Description Postulate

\rho(\Gamma)\;\geq\;0,\hskip 9.24994pt\int\rho(\Gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma\;=\;1.

All microstates are assigned a nonnegative probability density
\rho(\Gamma)
, normalized to unity.
Any measurable macroscopic quantity (observable) is given by

\langle A\rangle\;=\;\int A(\Gamma)\,\rho(\Gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

3.

Stationary Distribution for Equilibrium

\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho}{\mathrm{d}t}\;=\;0\hskip 9.24994pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994pt\rho(\Gamma)\text{ is time-invariant along Hamiltonian trajectories.}

In an equilibrium regime, the probability distribution remains constant in time when viewed in the appropriate phase-space representation.

4.

Ergodic Hypothesis

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{0}^{\tau}A\bigl(\Gamma(t)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\;=\;\int A(\Gamma)\,\rho(\Gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma,

for any phase function
A(\Gamma)
. The time average of an observable along a single long trajectory equals the ensemble average over the phase-space distribution. In particular, when
A
is the Hamiltonian, we get the internal energy of the system.

5.

Statistical Entropy Postulate

S[\rho]\;=\;-\,k_{\mathrm{B}}\int\rho(\Gamma)\,\ln\bigl[\rho(\Gamma)\bigr]\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

This defines the entropy as a functional of the probability distribution
\rho(\Gamma)
. In equilibrium ensembles,
S[\rho]
reduces to the thermodynamic entropy.

6.

Statistical Second Law

\Delta S[\rho]\;\geq\;0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for an isolated system.}

The entropy functional
S[\rho]
cannot decrease over time when the system is isolated (no exchange of energy or particles), reflecting the irreversible increase in macroscopic entropy.

7.

Statistical Third Law

\lim_{T\to 0}S[\rho_{\mathrm{eq}}]\;=\;\text{constant (often taken as zero)}.

As temperature
T\to 0
, the equilibrium distribution collapses to a minimal-entropy ground state. The statistical entropy thus approaches a constant, mirroring the thermodynamic third law.

J.2.2
Completeness

1.

Microscopic Foundations.
(
Axioms 1, 2
) define the phase-space setting and the probabilistic nature of macroscopic quantities.

2.

Equilibrium and Dynamics.
(
Axioms 3, 4
) ensure that we can identify equilibrium states (stationary distributions) and relate time averages to ensemble averages (ergodic hypothesis).

3.

Entropy.
(
Axiom 5
) completes the link between the probability distribution and the thermodynamic concept of entropy.

4.

Statistical Analogs of Thermodynamic Laws.
(
Axioms 6, 7
)
guarantee non-decreasing entropy (second law), and specify the limiting entropy at zero temperature (third law).

These axioms together allow one to construct the canonical, microcanonical, and grand canonical ensembles and to derive relationships between measurable thermodynamic quantities.

J.2.3
Independence

1.

Phase Space Representation (Axiom 1)

is not derivable from probabilistic or equilibrium arguments. It establishes the fundamental structure of microstates.

2.

Probability Postulate (Axiom 2)

does not follow merely from the existence of a phase space; it specifies how probabilities are assigned to microstates.

3.

Equilibrium Stationarity (Axiom 3)

must be separately stated; it cannot be inferred from the shape of
\rho(\Gamma)
alone without the notion of time invariance in Hamiltonian systems.

4.

Ergodic Hypothesis (Axiom 4)

is not implied by any combination of the first three axioms; it specifically identifies long-time behavior with ensemble statistics.

5.

Statistical Entropy (Axiom 5)

introduces a functional
S[\rho]
, which is not deducible solely from Axioms 1–4.

6.

Statistical Second Law (Axiom 6)

cannot be proved from earlier axioms alone; it asserts the non-decreasing nature of the entropy functional in isolated systems.

7.

Statistical Third Law (Axiom 7)

specifically addresses the behavior of
S[\rho]
as
T\to 0
, thus requiring a separate statement not implied by the others.

J.3
Transformation

J.3.1
Axiom 1

Goal:
Transform a subset of the classical thermodynamics axioms

\bigl\{\,T_{2},\,T_{3}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{aligned} T_{2}&:\text{First Law: }\mathrm{d}U=\delta Q-\delta W,\\
T_{3}&:\text{Second Law: }\Delta S=\int\frac{\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}}{T}\;\;\;(\text{reversible}),\;\;\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0\;(\text{general case}),\end{aligned}

into the
Statistical Mechanics
statement:

\text{SM}_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{``A system with }f\text{ degrees of freedom
is represented by a point in a }

2f\text{-dimensional phase space.''}

We achieve this through small symbolic rewrites, showing how macroscopic notions of energy and entropy guide us toward a microscopic (phase-space) description.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Thermodynamics, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,T_{2},\,T_{3}\bigr\}}

where

T_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{d}U=\delta Q-\delta W,\hskip 9.24994ptT_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S=\int\frac{\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}}{T},\;\;\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0.

Here,
T_{2}
says there is a well-defined internal energy
U
whose change depends on heat and work, while
T_{3}
introduces the state function entropy
S
, with
\Delta S
determined by reversible heat exchange over temperature, and non-decreasing for isolated systems.

Rewrite Step 1: Express Energy and Entropy
as Possible Functions of Micro-Configurations.

(T_{2},\,T_{3})\;\longrightarrow\;(T_{2}^{\prime},\,T_{3}^{\prime})

We restate
T_{2}
and
T_{3}
so that
U
and
S
may arise from more fundamental “micro-variables.” Denote each (unseen) microscopic configuration by a parameter set
\Gamma
. Symbolically:

T_{2}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994ptU=U(\Gamma),\hskip 18.49988ptT_{3}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994ptS=S(\Gamma).

Here we do not specify how
\Gamma
is structured, only that each macrostate (characterized by
U
and
S
) can be traced back to some underlying microstate label
\Gamma
.

S_{0}=\{\,T_{2},\,T_{3}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,T_{2}^{\prime},\,T_{3}^{\prime}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\bigl\{T_{2}^{\prime}:U(\Gamma),\;T_{3}^{\prime}:S(\Gamma)\bigr\}.}

We have replaced purely macroscopic notions with the idea that
U
and
S
can depend on a more microscopic description
\Gamma
.

Rewrite Step 2: Interpret
\Gamma

as Coordinates and Momenta (Degrees of Freedom).

(T_{2}^{\prime},\,T_{3}^{\prime})\;\longrightarrow\;(M_{1})

We let:

\Gamma\;=\;\bigl(q_{1},\dots,q_{f};\;p_{1},\dots,p_{f}\bigr).

Hence each microstate is a specification of generalized coordinates
(q_{i})
and momenta
(p_{i})
for the
f
degrees of freedom in the system. Symbolically:

M_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma=(q_{i},p_{i}),\hskip 9.24994pti=1,\dots,f.

Thus
U(\Gamma)
and
S(\Gamma)
become functions of
(q_{i},p_{i})
, linking thermodynamic potentials to a mechanical (Hamiltonian) representation.

S_{1}=\{\,T_{2}^{\prime},T_{3}^{\prime}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,M_{1}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,M_{1}:\,\Gamma=(q_{i},p_{i})\bigr\}.}

We interpret the microstate
\Gamma
explicitly as a combination of
2f
variables:
q_{i}
and
p_{i}
.

Rewrite Step 3: Collect All
(q_{i},p_{i})
into a Phase Space.

M_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;\text{SM}_{1}.

By considering the set of all possible
(q_{1},\dots,q_{f},p_{1},\dots,p_{f})
, we form a
2f
-dimensional
phase space
, in which each point
\Gamma
identifies one complete microstate of the system. Symbolically:

\text{SM}_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\;\in\;\Gamma_{\text{phase}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2f}.

This is precisely the
State Space (Phase Space) Postulate
in statistical mechanics:

“A system with
f
degrees of freedom
is represented by a point in a

2f
-dimensional phase space.”

Hence,

S_{2}=\{\,M_{1}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\text{SM}_{1}\}.

Final Set (First Axiom of Statistical Mechanics):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{\text{SM}_{1}:\,\Gamma\in\Gamma_{\text{phase}},\;\dim(\Gamma_{\text{phase}})=2f\Bigr\}.}

We conclude that every configuration of a system’s
f
degrees of freedom can be seen as a point in a
2f
-dimensional space, linking macroscopic thermodynamic functions
U,S
to underlying microscopic coordinates and momenta.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View):

\underbrace{\{\,T_{2},\,T_{3}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,T_{2}^{\prime},\,T_{3}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,M_{1}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\text{SM}_{1}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Thus, thermodynamic concepts of energy and entropy (
T_{2},T_{3}
) guide us to conceive a microscopic landscape
\Gamma
(step by step), culminating in the phase-space description
(Axiom 1 of Statistical Mechanics)
.

J.3.2
Axiom 2

Goal:
Transform a subset of the classical thermodynamics axioms

\bigl\{\,T_{3},\;T_{4}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{aligned} T_{3}&:\text{Second Law: }\Delta S=\int\frac{\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}}{T},\;\;\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0,\\
T_{4}&:\text{Third Law: }\lim_{T\to 0}S=S_{0},\end{aligned}

into the
Statistical Mechanics
statement:

\text{SM}_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\rho(\Gamma)\;\geq\;0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\int\rho(\Gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma\;=\;1,

often called the
Probabilistic Description Postulate.

The key idea is to connect entropy constraints in thermodynamics with a nonnegative, normalized distribution over microstates.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Thermodynamics, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,T_{3},\;T_{4}\bigr\}}

where

T_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S=\int\frac{\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}}{T}\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{for a reversible process}),\;\;\;\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{general case}),

T_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\lim_{T\to 0}S=S_{0}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{(a constant for a perfectly ordered system).}

Here,
T_{3}
asserts that entropy
S
is defined by reversible heat exchange over temperature and cannot decrease in an isolated system, while
T_{4}
concerns the entropy’s limiting behavior at zero temperature.

Rewrite Step 1: Connect Entropy to Counting or Measuring Microstates.

(T_{3},\,T_{4})\;\longrightarrow\;(T_{3}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime})

Following a standard microscopic interpretation, we restate:

T_{3}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S\geq 0\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\text{the measure of accessible microstates }

(in an isolated system) cannot decrease.

T_{4}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994ptS\to S_{0}\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\text{the number (or measure) of microstates collapses}

\text{to a minimal set as }T\to 0.

Symbolically,
T_{3}^{\prime}
implies that an isolated system evolves toward states with maximal effective microstate measure;
T_{4}^{\prime}
indicates that at zero temperature, the system has minimal possible entropy, suggesting vanishing configurational multiplicity in the ground state (or a similarly limited set).

S_{0}=\{\,T_{3},\,T_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,T_{3}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\Bigl\{\,T_{3}^{\prime}:\underbrace{\text{``\#(microstates) can't decrease"}}_{\Delta S\geq 0},\;T_{4}^{\prime}:\underbrace{\text{''\# (microstates) collapses to a minimal set as }T\to 0"}_{S\to S_{0}}\Bigr\}.}

These reinterpret thermodynamic entropy constraints as statements about the measure of microstates.

Rewrite Step 2: Introduce a Nonnegative Weight for Each Microstate.

(T_{3}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime})\;\longrightarrow\;(M_{2})

To quantify “number of microstates,” assign a nonnegative weight
w(\Gamma)\geq 0
to each microstate
\Gamma
. In equilibrium ensemble theory,
w(\Gamma)
might later be related to Boltzmann factors, but we do not assume that form yet. We only assert:

M_{2}:\hskip 9.24994ptw(\Gamma)\,\geq\,0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all}\;\Gamma,

so that the total measure (or “count”) of microstates is

\int w(\Gamma)\,d\Gamma
.
Thus,
\Delta S\geq 0
corresponds to the statement that in an isolated system, this measure cannot spontaneously decrease, connecting with the Second Law perspective.

S_{1}=\{\,T_{3}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,M_{2}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\Bigl\{\,M_{2}:\;w(\Gamma)\geq 0\Bigr\}.}

We introduce a generic, nonnegative weight over microstates, representing how accessible or populated each microstate can be.

Rewrite Step 3: Normalize the Weights to Form a Probability Distribution.

M_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;\text{SM}_{2}.

Since a physical system must
occupy
exactly one microstate at a time, and assuming the total measure is finite or normalizable, we write

\int w(\Gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma\;=\;C>0.

Dividing by
C
, define

\rho(\Gamma)\;=\;\frac{w(\Gamma)}{C},

which satisfies

\rho(\Gamma)\;\geq\;0,\hskip 9.24994pt\int\rho(\Gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma=1.

This is the
Probabilistic Description Postulate
, stating that ensemble averages of observables
A(\Gamma)
are given by

\langle A\rangle\;=\;\int A(\Gamma)\,\rho(\Gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

Hence,

S_{2}=\{\,M_{2}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\,\text{SM}_{2}:\;\rho(\Gamma)\geq 0,\;\;\int\rho(\Gamma)\,d\Gamma=1\}.

Final Set (Second Axiom of Statistical Mechanics, Probabilistic Description):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{\text{SM}_{2}:\,\rho(\Gamma)\geq 0,\;\;\int\rho(\Gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma=1\Bigr\}.}

Physical observables are computed by averaging a phase-space function over this probability density. Nonnegativity captures the idea that the measure of microstates cannot be negative, and normalization reflects the fact that the system must be in
some
microstate with probability
1
.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View):

\underbrace{\{\,T_{3},\,T_{4}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,T_{3}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,M_{2}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\text{SM}_{2}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Each step refines the statement “entropy constraints” into a requirement that each microstate carry a nonnegative weight, ultimately normalized to form a
probability distribution
over microstates, thus yielding
Axiom 2 of Statistical Mechanics
.

J.3.3
Axiom 3

Goal:
Transform a subset of the classical thermodynamics axioms

\bigl\{\,T_{1},\;T_{3}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{aligned} T_{1}&:\text{Zeroth Law: Thermal Equilibrium is well-defined},\\
T_{3}&:\text{Second Law: }\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0,\end{aligned}

into the
Statistical Mechanics
statement:

\text{SM}_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho}{\mathrm{d}t}\;=\;0\hskip 9.24994pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994pt\rho(\Gamma)\;\text{is time-invariant along Hamiltonian trajectories (equilibrium).}

The idea is to show that once a system is in equilibrium (from a macroscopic perspective), the probability distribution
\rho(\Gamma)
must also be stationary in time.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Thermodynamics, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,T_{1},\,T_{3}\bigr\}}

where

T_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{If two systems are in thermal equilibrium with a third, they share}

equilibrium with each other.

T_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{(entropy of an isolated system never decreases).}

Here,
T_{1}
establishes a well-defined notion of thermal equilibrium (common temperature), while
T_{3}
ensures an isolated system moves toward a maximum-entropy state.

Rewrite Step 1: Single Equilibrium State at the Macroscopic Level.

(T_{1},\,T_{3})\;\longrightarrow\;(T_{1}^{\prime},\,T_{3}^{\prime})

We restate:

T_{1}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{There exists a unique (or stable) macroscopic equilibrium condition,}

characterized by a uniform temperature
T
.

T_{3}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{An isolated system spontaneously evolves toward that equilibrium,}

reaching maximal entropy (no further increase possible).

Hence,
T_{1}^{\prime}
asserts the existence of equilibrium, and
T_{3}^{\prime}
says once the system has arrived there, it remains in that state (since no net entropy production occurs further).

S_{0}=\{\,T_{1},\,T_{3}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,T_{1}^{\prime},\,T_{3}^{\prime}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\,T_{1}^{\prime},T_{3}^{\prime}\}.}

Where

T_{1}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Well-defined equilibrium temperature}

T_{3}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{System evolves irreversibly to that equilibrium.}

In simpler terms: once at macroscopic equilibrium, there’s no further spontaneous change in thermodynamic variables.

Rewrite Step 2: Equilibrium Implies No Change in Macroscopic Observables Over Time.

(T_{1}^{\prime},\,T_{3}^{\prime})\;\longrightarrow\;(M_{3})

At equilibrium, observable thermodynamic quantities (e.g.
U,P,T,S
) stop changing with time:

M_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\mathrm{d}(\text{Macrostate})}{\mathrm{d}t}=0\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{time-invariance of macroscopic variables}).

Symbolically,
M_{3}
expresses that once equilibrium is established, the system’s measured properties are stationary on the macroscopic level.

S_{1}=\{\,T_{1}^{\prime},\,T_{3}^{\prime}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,M_{3}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,M_{3}:\text{No net change in macroscopic variables at equilibrium}\}.}

We are still describing equilibrium in purely thermodynamic (macroscopic) terms.

Rewrite Step 3: Relate Macroscopic Stationarity to a Time-Invariant Probability Distribution.

M_{3}\;\longrightarrow\;\text{SM}_{3}.

Statistical mechanics connects macroscopic observables
X
(energy, entropy, etc.) to phase-space integrals over a probability distribution
\rho(\Gamma)
:

X\;=\;\int X(\Gamma)\,\rho(\Gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

If
X
does not change with time, it must be that
\rho(\Gamma,t)
ceases to evolve:

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\bigl(\text{any observable}\bigr)\;=\;0\hskip 9.24994pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho}{\mathrm{d}t}=0\;\;(\text{in the appropriate representation}).

Hence, at equilibrium,

\rho(\Gamma)\text{ is time-invariant along Hamiltonian trajectories.}

We denote this as

\text{SM}_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho}{\mathrm{d}t}=0\;\Longrightarrow\;\rho(\Gamma)\text{ is stationary}.

Thus,

S_{2}=\{\,M_{3}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\text{SM}_{3}\}.

Final Set (Third Axiom of Statistical Mechanics, Stationary Distribution):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{\text{SM}_{3}:\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho}{\mathrm{d}t}=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\rho(\Gamma)\,\text{time-invariant in equilibrium}.\Bigr\}.}

Equilibrium in the thermodynamic sense translates to a
stationary

\rho(\Gamma)
in statistical mechanics.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View):

\underbrace{\{\,T_{1},\,T_{3}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,T_{1}^{\prime},\,T_{3}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,M_{3}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\text{SM}_{3}\}}_{S_{3}}.

We see that defining equilibrium macroscopically (no change in thermodynamic state) implies a
stationary
probability distribution at the microscopic level, giving us
Axiom 3 of Statistical Mechanics
.

J.3.4
Axiom 4

Goal:
Transform a subset of the classical thermodynamics axioms

\bigl\{\,T_{1},\;T_{4}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{aligned} T_{1}&:\text{Zeroth Law: thermal equilibrium is well-defined},\\
T_{4}&:\text{Third Law: }\lim_{T\to 0}S=S_{0},\end{aligned}

into the
Statistical Mechanics
statement:

\text{SM}_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{0}^{\tau}A\bigl(\Gamma(t)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\;=\;\int A(\Gamma)\,\rho(\Gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma,

known as the
Ergodic Hypothesis
: long-time averages equal ensemble averages.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Thermodynamics, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,T_{1},\,T_{4}\bigr\}}

where

T_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Thermal transitivity.}

T_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\lim_{T\to 0}S=S_{0}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{(the entropy approaches a constant at zero temperature).}

T_{1}
indicates the well-definedness of equilibrium temperature, while
T_{4}
states that as
T\to 0
, the system’s entropy goes to a minimum. Although these do not
directly
require ergodicity, they imply (in practice) that equilibrium ensembles and ground states can be consistently described for all temperatures.

Rewrite Step 1: Single Temperature for the Entire System Implies
a Unified Ensemble Description.

(T_{1},\,T_{4})\;\longrightarrow\;(T_{1}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime})

We restate:

T_{1}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{All parts of the system share one temperature parameter $T$, ensuring a single ensemble.}

T_{4}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{As }T\to 0,\;S\!\to\!S_{0}\text{ , implying a collapse to a ground-state microconfiguration.}

Hence,
T_{1}^{\prime}
says the system in equilibrium can be described by one temperature-based ensemble, and
T_{4}^{\prime}
emphasizes that in the low-temperature limit, the system explores only a narrow subset of microstates.

S_{0}=\{\,T_{1},\,T_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,T_{1}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\,T_{1}^{\prime},T_{4}^{\prime}\}.}

Where

T_{1}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Single temperature unifies the ensemble},

T_{4}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Narrow microstate distribution at $T\to 0$}.

We have recast
T_{1}
and
T_{4}
to emphasize the existence of a single thermal ensemble at any
T
, and the near-collapse of that ensemble at very low
T
.

Rewrite Step 2: Introduce Microstate Trajectories.

(T_{1}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime})\;\longrightarrow\;(M_{4})

From statistical mechanics, a single system with temperature
T>0
explores many microstates
\Gamma
over time. Denote its phase-space trajectory by
\Gamma(t)
. Symbolically:

M_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma(t)\;\text{is the system's microscopic path under Hamiltonian dynamics.}

Although
T_{4}^{\prime}
indicates that at
T\to 0
, the system collapses to a small set of microstates (like a ground state), for
T>0
we expect it to explore multiple states given enough time.

S_{1}=\{\,T_{1}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,M_{4}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\bigl\{M_{4}:\,\Gamma(t)\text{ is a phase-space trajectory of the system over time.}\bigr\}.}

We now have an explicit model of microscopic time evolution, consistent with the notion that a nonzero temperature implies sampling of various states.

Rewrite Step 3: Identify Long-Time Macroscopic Equilibrium
with Equal Sampling of Accessible States.

M_{4}\;\longrightarrow\;\text{SM}_{4}.

The
Ergodic Hypothesis
says that for an equilibrium system with distribution
\rho(\Gamma)
, the fraction of time spent in a region of phase space matches
\rho(\Gamma)
for that region. Formally, for any observable
A(\Gamma)
,

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{0}^{\tau}A\bigl(\Gamma(t)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\;=\;\int A(\Gamma)\,\rho(\Gamma)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

We denote:

\text{SM}_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\bigl(\text{Time average of }A\bigr)=\bigl(\text{Ensemble average of }A\bigr).

Thus,
over long times
, a single system trajectory
\Gamma(t)

represents
the entire ensemble. This is not demanded directly by
T_{1}
or
T_{4}
in thermodynamics, but it is
consistent
with the idea that any equilibrium system at positive temperature will, in principle, explore its accessible microstates.

Hence,

S_{2}=\{\,M_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\text{SM}_{4}\}.

Final Set (Fourth Axiom of Statistical Mechanics, Ergodic Hypothesis):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{\text{SM}_{4}:\,\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\tfrac{1}{\tau}\int_{0}^{\tau}A(\Gamma(t))\,dt=\int A(\Gamma)\,\rho(\Gamma)\,d\Gamma\Bigr\}.}

Time averages of observables equal ensemble averages under equilibrium conditions.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View):

\underbrace{\{\,T_{1},\,T_{4}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,T_{1}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,M_{4}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\text{SM}_{4}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Thus, while
T_{1}
and
T_{4}
do not by themselves demand ergodicity, they strongly support the premise of a single ensemble at finite temperature and a minimal microstate set at
T\to 0
. Interpreted in a phase-space framework, this naturally leads to the notion that the system’s long-time trajectory explores the ensemble in such a way that
time
averages and
ensemble
averages coincide, yielding the
Ergodic Hypothesis
.

J.3.5
Axiom 5

Goal:
Transform a subset of the classical thermodynamics axioms

\bigl\{\,T_{3},\;T_{4}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{aligned} T_{3}&:\text{Second Law: }\Delta S=\int\frac{\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}}{T},\;\;\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0,\\
T_{4}&:\text{Third Law: }\lim_{T\to 0}S=S_{0},\end{aligned}

into the
Statistical Mechanics
statement:

\text{SM}_{5}:\hskip 9.24994ptS[\rho]\;=\;-\,k_{\mathrm{B}}\int\rho(\Gamma)\,\ln\bigl[\rho(\Gamma)\bigr]\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,

the
Statistical Entropy Postulate
, linking the thermodynamic entropy
S
with the probability distribution
\rho(\Gamma)
.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Thermodynamics, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,T_{3},\;T_{4}\bigr\}}

where

T_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S=\int\frac{\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}}{T}\;\;(\text{reversible}),\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0\;\;(\text{general}),

T_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\lim_{T\to 0}S=S_{0}\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{entropy goes to a constant at }T\to 0).

Here,
T_{3}
defines entropy as a state function (increasing for irreversible processes in isolated systems), and
T_{4}
specifies its limiting value at low temperature.

Rewrite Step 1: Interpret Entropy as a Measure of Accessible Microstates.

(T_{3},\,T_{4})\;\longrightarrow\;(T_{3}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime})

We restate:

T_{3}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Entropy $S$ measures the ``size'' (or measure) of the set of accessible }

\text{microstates, with }\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0.

T_{4}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\lim_{T\to 0}S\;=\;S_{0}\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;\text{the system occupies a minimal set at zero temperature.}

Symbolically,
T_{3}^{\prime}
emphasizes that larger sets of microstates correspond to higher entropy (and that entropy cannot spontaneously decrease), while
T_{4}^{\prime}
ensures the measure collapses to a minimal subset in the
T\to 0
limit.

S_{0}=\{\,T_{3},\,T_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,T_{3}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\Bigl\{T_{3}^{\prime},T_{4}^{\prime}\Bigr\}.}

Where

T_{3}^{\prime}:\text{``$S$ is a measure of microstates (non-decreasing in isolation)''}

T_{4}^{\prime}:\text{``$S\to S_{0}$ as $T\to 0$''}

We now express entropy as intimately tied to the “measure” or effective count of microstates, with a vanishing degeneracy at zero temperature.

Rewrite Step 2: Connect This “Measure of Microstates” to a Probability Distribution
\rho(\Gamma)
.

(T_{3}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime})\;\longrightarrow\;(M_{5})

Recall from previous axioms that each microstate
\Gamma
has a probability density
\rho(\Gamma)\geq 0
, normalized to 1. Thus,
S_{1}
suggests that:

\Delta S\geq 0\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\text{the distribution $\rho(\Gamma)$ spreads or remains the same,}

never shrinking onto fewer states for an isolated system.

Similarly,
S\to S_{0}
as
T\to 0
implies
\rho(\Gamma)
shrinks toward a minimal set. Symbolically:

M_{5}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{``Entropy $S$ must be expressible as a functional of $\rho(\Gamma)$ reflecting how widely $\rho$ spreads.''}

We do not yet specify the
form
of that functional.

S_{1}=\{\,T_{3}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,M_{5}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\bigl\{M_{5}:\,S\text{ is a (still-unknown) functional }S[\rho(\Gamma)]\text{ reflecting microstate spread.}\bigr\}.}

We know
S
depends on
\rho
but have not yet specified the exact dependence.

Rewrite Step 3: Identify the Unique Functional
S[\rho]
Satisfying
T_{3}^{\prime},T_{4}^{\prime}
.

M_{5}\;\longrightarrow\;\text{SM}_{5}.

From information theory and thermodynamics arguments, there is a well-known
unique (up to additive/scale constants)
functional that:

1.

Increases when
\rho
becomes more “spread out” (no microstates are removed; partial mixing or spreading always raises entropy).

2.

Goes to a minimal value when
\rho
is concentrated on as few microstates as possible (consistent with
T\to 0
).

3.

Is additive over independent subsystems (extensivity).

These conditions identify the Gibbs/Shannon form:

\text{SM}_{5}:\hskip 9.24994ptS[\rho]\;=\;-\,k_{\mathrm{B}}\int\rho(\Gamma)\,\ln\bigl[\rho(\Gamma)\bigr]\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,

where
k_{\mathrm{B}}
is Boltzmann’s constant (fixing units). This formula reproduces the classical thermodynamic entropy under equilibrium ensembles and obeys
T_{3}^{\prime},T_{4}^{\prime}
in limiting cases.

Hence,

S_{2}=\{\,M_{5}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\bigl\{\,\text{SM}_{5}:\,S[\rho]=-k_{\mathrm{B}}\!\int\rho\ln\rho\,\bigr\}.

Final Set (Fifth Axiom of Statistical Mechanics, Statistical Entropy):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{\text{SM}_{5}:\,S[\rho]\;=\;-\,k_{\mathrm{B}}\int\rho(\Gamma)\,\ln\!\bigl[\rho(\Gamma)\bigr]\mathrm{d}\Gamma\Bigr\}.}

This connects the thermodynamic entropy
S
to a precise functional of the probability distribution
\rho(\Gamma)
. In equilibrium ensembles (e.g. canonical, microcanonical),
S[\rho]
reduces to the familiar thermodynamic entropy, consistent with
T_{3}
and
T_{4}
.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View):

\underbrace{\{\,T_{3},\,T_{4}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,T_{3}^{\prime},\,T_{4}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,M_{5}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\text{SM}_{5}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Thus, by reinterpreting thermodynamic constraints on
S
(increase under isolation, limiting value at
T\to 0
) as properties of a microstate distribution
\rho
, we derive the familiar
Gibbs-Shannon formula
for statistical entropy.

J.3.6
Axiom 6

Goal:
Transform a subset of the classical thermodynamics axioms

\bigl\{\,T_{3}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{aligned} T_{3}&:\text{Second Law: }\Delta S=\int\frac{\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}}{T},\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0,\end{aligned}

into the
Statistical Mechanics
statement:

\text{SM}_{6}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S[\rho]\;\geq\;0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for an isolated system},

the statistical version of the second law of thermodynamics, asserting that the entropy functional does not decrease in an isolated system.

Initial Axiom from Classical Thermodynamics:

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,T_{3}\bigr\}}

where

T_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S=\int\frac{\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}}{T}\;\;(\text{reversible process}),\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0\;\;(\text{general case}).

This states that entropy
S
is non-decreasing for an isolated system and is defined via
\delta Q_{\mathrm{rev}}/T
for reversible transformations.

Rewrite Step 1: Interpret the Non-Decreasing Entropy as Growth in Microstate Measure.

T_{3}\;\longrightarrow\;T_{3}^{\prime}

We restate the Second Law in microstate language:

T_{3}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\text{the measure (or accessible set) of microstates }

\text{does not decrease over time}.

Symbolically, an isolated system evolves so that its microstate distribution does not spontaneously shrink onto fewer states, reflecting an
irreversible
spreading or mixing of configurations.

S_{0}=\{\,T_{3}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,T_{3}^{\prime}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\Bigl\{T_{3}^{\prime}:\,\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;\text{no net loss of microstates}\Bigr\}.}

We have translated the classical thermodynamic statement into a microstate-centered perspective of entropy increase.

Rewrite Step 2: Apply the Entropy Functional
S[\rho]

from the Statistical Entropy Postulate.

T_{3}^{\prime}\;\longrightarrow\;M_{6}

Recall from
Axiom 5
(Statistical Entropy Postulate) that the entropy for a distribution
\rho(\Gamma)
is

S[\rho]\;=\;-\,k_{\mathrm{B}}\int\rho(\Gamma)\,\ln\bigl[\rho(\Gamma)\bigr]\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

Then,
T_{3}^{\prime}
(“microstates do not decrease for an isolated system”) implies that
S[\rho]
cannot decrease in an isolated process. Symbolically:

M_{6}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S[\rho]\;\geq\;0\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{for an isolated system evolving in time}).

Hence,
M_{7}
encodes the second law into a statement about the functional
S[\rho]
.

S_{1}=\{\,T_{3}^{\prime}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,M_{6}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\Bigl\{M_{6}:\,\Delta S[\rho]\geq 0\;(\text{isolated system}),\;\text{where }S[\rho]\text{ is }-k_{\mathrm{B}}\!\int\rho\ln\rho\,d\Gamma\Bigr\}.}

We now have a direct statement linking the classical second law to a variation in
S[\rho]
over time.

Rewrite Step 3: Conclude the Statistical Second Law.

M_{6}\;\longrightarrow\;\text{SM}_{6}.

Finally, we identify this condition as the
Statistical Second Law
:

\text{SM}_{6}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta S[\rho]\;\geq\;0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for any isolated system.}

Thus, if
\rho(\Gamma,t)
evolves under isolated Hamiltonian dynamics, the functional
S[\rho(t)]
cannot decrease, matching the classical statement that
\Delta S_{\mathrm{isolated}}\geq 0
.

Hence,

S_{2}=\{\,M_{6}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\bigl\{\,\text{SM}_{6}:\,\Delta S[\rho]\geq 0\bigr\}.

Final Set (Statistical Second Law):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{\text{SM}_{6}:\,\Delta S[\rho]\;\geq\;0\;\text{ for an isolated system}\Bigr\}.}

Here,
S[\rho]
is the same entropy functional from Axiom 5, and this axiom guarantees it never decreases for an isolated system, mirroring the classical thermodynamic second law.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View):

\underbrace{\{\,T_{3}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,T_{3}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,M_{6}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\text{SM}_{6}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Thus, the
Statistical Second Law
emerges from the classical second law (
T_{3}
) and the notion that entropy corresponds to a monotonic functional of
\rho(\Gamma)
(Axiom 5). In an isolated system,
\Delta S[\rho]\geq 0
completes the parallel to classical irreversibility.

J.3.7
Axiom 7

Goal:
Transform a subset of the classical thermodynamics axioms

\bigl\{\,T_{4}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994ptT_{4}:\lim_{T\to 0}S\;=\;S_{0},

into the
Statistical Mechanics
statement, an equivalent of the third law of thermodynamics:

\text{SM}_{7}:\hskip 9.24994pt\lim_{T\to 0}S[\rho_{\mathrm{eq}}]\;=\;\text{constant (often taken as zero)}.

Initial Axiom from Classical Thermodynamics:

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,T_{4}\bigr\}}

where

T_{4}:\lim_{T\to 0}S\;=\;S_{0},

This states that entropy
S
is approaches a constant value as the temperature tends to zero.

Rewrite Step 1: Introduce the statistical notion of entropy

T_{4}\;\longrightarrow\;SM_{7}:\hskip 9.24994pt\lim_{T\to 0}S[\rho_{\mathrm{eq}}]\;=\;\text{constant (often taken as zero)}.

We simply change the classical notion of entropy to its statistical equivalent (a functional of the probability distribution).

S_{0}=\{\,T_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,SM_{7}\}.

Final Set (Statistical Third Law):

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\ SM_{7}:\hskip 9.24994pt\lim_{T\to 0}S[\rho_{\mathrm{eq}}]\;=\;\text{constant (often taken as zero)}.\}\ }

Here,
S[\rho]
is the same entropy functional from Axiom 5, and this axiom describes its behavior as temperature goes to zero.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View):

\underbrace{\{\,T_{4}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,SM_{7}\}}_{S_{1}}

Thus, the statistical third law emerges from the classical third law (
T_{3}
) and the notion that entropy corresponds to a monotonic functional of
\rho(\Gamma)
.

Appendix K
From Newtonian mechanics to Hamiltonian mechanics

K.0.1
Classical/Newtonian Physics

The axioms are the same as defined in Section
I.1
.

K.1
Hamiltonian Mechanics

Hamiltonian mechanics reformulates classical mechanics using a

phase space
—commonly
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
—whose coordinates
are generalized positions and momenta. A single scalar function (the
Hamiltonian) governs the system’s time evolution through Hamilton’s
equations. This formalism offers deep insights into conservation laws,
symplectic geometry, and unifies the treatment of diverse classical systems.

K.1.1
Axioms

1.

Phase Space (Axiom 1)

\text{A system is described by a }2n\text{-dimensional phase space }(q_{i},p_{i}).

All possible states correspond to points in this space.

2.

Hamiltonian Function (Axiom 2)

H=H(q_{i},p_{i}),

which encodes the total energy (often
T+V
) in terms of positions
and momenta.

3.

Hamilton’s Equations (Axiom 3)

\dot{q}_{i}\,=\,\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}},\hskip 9.24994pt\dot{p}_{i}\,=\,-\,\frac{\partial H}{\partial q_{i}}.

These specify how the system evolves in time.

K.1.2
Completeness

1.

Phase Space (Axiom 1)
provides the mathematical arena:
each point
(q_{i},p_{i})
specifies a complete state.

2.

Hamiltonian Function (Axiom 2)
supplies a single scalar
that contains all the energetic information. From it, one derives the
equations of motion for both integrable and more general systems.

3.

Hamilton’s Equations (Axiom 3)
determine the unique
time evolution of
(q_{i},p_{i})
. Together with the Hamiltonian,
they enable predictions of trajectories and conserved quantities in
phase space.

Hence, these three axioms suffice to describe classical mechanics in
Hamilton’s formalism, covering a wide range of physical phenomena from
simple particles to complex, multi-degree-of-freedom systems.

K.1.3
Independence

1.

Phase Space
cannot be inferred from the existence of a
Hamiltonian or the dynamical equations alone. One must explicitly
posit a
2n
-dimensional space of
(q_{i},p_{i})
.

2.

Hamiltonian Function
does not automatically follow from
the notion of phase space or from the form of Hamilton’s equations.
It is the unique scalar whose partial derivatives produce the system’s
time evolution.

3.

Hamilton’s Equations
themselves are not implied by
merely specifying
(q_{i},p_{i})
and an energy function. They must be
taken as the prescription for how states in phase space evolve with time.

None of the three can be derived from the others, ensuring their mutual
independence.

K.2
Transformation

K.2.1
Axiom 1

Goal:

Obtain the
first Hamiltonian Axiom
:

H_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{The state of a system is a point in a }2n\text{-dimensional
phase space }(q_{i},p_{i}).

Starting from a subset of Newton’s Axioms:

S_{0}=\bigl\{\,N_{2},\;N_{3},\;N_{5}\bigr\},

where

N_{2}:\;\text{Inertial frames},\hskip 9.24994ptN_{3}:\;\vec{F}=m\,\dfrac{d\vec{v}}{dt},\hskip 9.24994ptN_{5}:\;\vec{F}=\sum_{i}\vec{F_{i}}\;(\text{superposition}).

Rewrite Step 1: Move from

\vec{F}=m\,\dfrac{d^{2}\vec{x}}{dt^{2}}

to Generalized Coordinates.

N_{3}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\bigl(\vec{x},\vec{v}\bigr)\;\mapsto\;(q_{i},\dot{q}_{i}).

In place of Cartesian
(\vec{x},\vec{v})
, we use
(q_{1},\dots,q_{n})
and
their time derivatives
\dot{q}_{i}
.

S_{0}=\{\,N_{2},N_{3},N_{5}\}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{1}=\{\,N_{2},N_{5},B_{1}\}.

Rewrite Step 2: Define Momentum and Form a
2n
-Dimensional Space.

B_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994ptp_{i}=m\,\dot{q}_{i}\;\;(\text{or }p_{i}=\tfrac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{q}_{i}}\text{ in general}).

Hence each
q_{i}
has a conjugate momentum
p_{i}
,
making the state
(q_{i},p_{i})
.

S_{1}=\{\,N_{2},N_{5},B_{1}\}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{2}=\{\,N_{2},N_{5},B_{2}\}.

Rewrite Step 3: Identify
(q_{i},p_{i})
as Canonical Coordinates.

(N_{2},\,N_{5},\,B_{2})\;\longrightarrow\;H_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{States occupy }(q_{i},p_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

Thus we rewrite Newtonian dynamics in
phase space
.

S_{2}=\{\,N_{2},N_{5},B_{2}\}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{3}=\{\,H_{1}\}.

Conclusion:

\boxed{H_{1}:\;\text{System state }=\bigl(q_{i},p_{i}\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}.}

We have transformed the Newtonian axioms into the statement that
a classical system is described by a
canonical phase space

of dimension
2n
.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View):

\underbrace{\{\,N_{2},\,N_{3},\,N_{5}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,N_{2},\,N_{5},\,B_{1}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,N_{2},\,N_{5},\,B_{2}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,H_{1}\}}_{S_{3}}.

K.2.2
Axiom 2

Starting Point:

We take the Newtonian axioms
\{N_{2},\,N_{3},\,N_{5}\}
together with

Axiom 1 (Phase Space)
:

\{\,N_{2},\,N_{3},\,N_{5}\}\;\longrightarrow\;\{\,H_{1}\},

as shown previously. Hence we begin here with

S_{0}=\{\,H_{1},\;N_{3},\;N_{5}\}.

We aim to formulate the
Hamiltonian
as a function of
(q_{i},p_{i})
,
the second axiom of Hamiltonian mechanics:

H_{2}:\hskip 9.24994ptH(q_{i},p_{i})\,\text{defines the total energy in terms of }(q_{i},p_{i}).

Rewrite Step 1: Identify Kinetic and Potential Energies.

B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994ptH(q,p)=T(p)+V(q),\hskip 9.24994ptT(p)=\frac{p^{2}}{2m},

By
N_{3}
(Newton’s second law) and
N_{5}
(superposition of forces) with
conservative forces, we express the total energy as kinetic plus potential. In principle,
V
can also depend on
\dot{q}
, but we omit this case in this transformation.

S_{0}=\{\,H_{1},N_{3},N_{5}\}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{1}=\{\,H_{1},B_{1}\}.

Rewrite Step 2: State
H(q,p)
as the Fundamental Energy Function.

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{``The scalar function }H(q,p)\text{ is the primary energy
for the system''}.

We now regard
H
itself (rather than
T
or
V
separately) as
the fundamental entity describing energy in phase space.

S_{1}=\{\,H_{1},B_{1}\}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{2}=\{\,H_{1},B_{2}\}.

Axiom 2 (Hamiltonian Function)

(H_{1},B_{2})\;\longrightarrow\;H_{2}:\hskip 9.24994ptH(q_{i},p_{i})\text{ defines total energy.}

Thus we obtain the
second Hamiltonian Axiom
:

\boxed{H_{2}:\;\text{The Hamiltonian }H(q_{i},p_{i})\text{ encodes all energy.}}

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View) for Axiom 2:

\underbrace{\{\,H_{1},\,N_{3},\,N_{5}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,H_{1},B_{1}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,H_{1},B_{2}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,H_{2}\}}_{\text{Axiom 2}}.

K.2.3
Axiom 3

Starting Point:

We now combine
\{H_{1},\,H_{2}\}
with
N_{3}
to obtain the

third Hamiltonian Axiom
:

H_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\dot{q}_{i}=\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}},\hskip 9.24994pt\dot{p}_{i}=-\,\frac{\partial H}{\partial q_{i}}.

S_{0}=\bigl\{\,H_{1},\;H_{2},\;N_{3}\bigr\}.

Rewrite Step (Direct):

H_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\dot{q}_{i}=\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}},\hskip 9.24994pt\dot{p}_{i}=-\frac{\partial H}{\partial q_{i}}.

Here, from
N_{3}
(Newton’s second law), if
H=p^{2}/(2m)+V(q)
, then

\dot{q}_{i}=\partial H/\partial p_{i}
and

\dot{p}_{i}=-\,\partial H/\partial q_{i}
.
This final step links classical force laws with the Hamiltonian
formulation
(H_{1},H_{2})
to yield Hamilton’s equations.

S_{0}=\{\,H_{1},H_{2},N_{3}\}\;\longrightarrow\;\{\,H_{3}\}.

Axiom 3 (Hamilton’s Equations)

\boxed{H_{3}:\;\dot{q}_{i}=\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}},\hskip 9.24994pt\dot{p}_{i}=-\,\frac{\partial H}{\partial q_{i}}.}

These canonical equations succinctly encode the system’s dynamics in
phase space.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View) for Axiom 3:

\underbrace{\{\,H_{1},H_{2},N_{3}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,H_{3}\}}_{S_{1}}.

Appendix L
From Hamiltonian mechanics to quantum mechanics

L.1
Hamiltonian Mechanics

The axioms are the same as those introduced in Sec.
K.1
.

L.2
Quantum Mechanics

Quantum mechanics is a fundamental theory that describes physical phenomena at the atomic and subatomic scales. It departs from classical (Newtonian) intuitions by emphasizing wave-particle duality, quantized energy levels, and the probabilistic nature of measurement outcomes. Quantum mechanics has been extraordinarily successful in explaining phenomena such as the structure of atoms, chemical bonding, and the behavior of elementary particles.

L.2.1
Axioms

1.

State Space (Hilbert Space)

\left|\psi\right>\in\mathcal{H},\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\psi|\psi\rangle=1,

where
\left|\psi\right>
is a (normalized) state vector in a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. All possible physical states of a system are represented by (equivalence classes of) vectors in
\mathcal{H}
.

2.

Observables as Self-Adjoint Operators

\hat{A}=\hat{A}^{\dagger},

Physical observables (e.g., position, momentum, energy) are represented by self-adjoint (Hermitian) operators
\hat{A}
on
\mathcal{H}
.

3.

Measurement Postulate (Born Rule)

P(\alpha_{i})=\bigl|\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle\bigr|^{2},

When measuring an observable
\hat{A}
with eigenvalues
\alpha_{i}
and corresponding eigenstates
\left|\phi_{i}\right>
, the probability of obtaining outcome
\alpha_{i}
is given by the squared amplitude of the state projected onto
\left|\phi_{i}\right>
.

4.

State Collapse

\left|\psi\right>\hskip 9.24994pt\xrightarrow{\text{measure }\hat{A}}\hskip 9.24994pt\left|\phi_{i}\right>,

Immediately after obtaining a specific measurement outcome
\alpha_{i}
, the system’s state “collapses” to the corresponding eigenstate
\left|\phi_{i}\right>
.

5.

Time Evolution (Schrödinger Equation)

i\hbar\,\frac{d}{dt}\left|\psi(t)\right>\;=\;\hat{H}\,\left|\psi(t)\right>,

Between measurements, a closed quantum system evolves unitarily under the Hamiltonian operator
\hat{H}
. The Schrödinger equation governs how the state vector changes in time.

6.

Intrinsic Spin

\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{2}\left|\psi\right>=\hbar^{2}s(s+1)\left|\psi\right>,\hskip 9.24994ptS_{z}\left|\psi\right>=\hbar m_{s}\left|\psi\right>,

In addition to orbital degrees of freedom, particles possess an intrinsic spin described by the spin operator
\hat{\mathbf{S}}
, which satisfies the standard angular-momentum commutation relations. The spin quantum number
s
can be integer or half-integer (
0,\tfrac{1}{2},1,\tfrac{3}{2},2,\dots
). For a general spin
s
,
m_{s}
takes values in
\{-s,-s+1,\dots,+s\}
.

7.

Exchange Symmetry and Pauli Principle

\Psi(\dots,\mathbf{r}_{i},\sigma_{i},\dots,\mathbf{r}_{j},\sigma_{j},\dots)=(-1)^{2s}\,\Psi(\dots,\mathbf{r}_{j},\sigma_{j},\dots,\mathbf{r}_{i},\sigma_{i},\dots),

Identical particles with spin
s
have a many-particle wavefunction
\Psi
that gains a factor of
(-1)^{2s}
upon exchanging any two such particles (including their spin variables
\sigma_{i}
). For integer
s
(bosons),
(-1)^{2s}=+1
and the wavefunction is symmetric under exchange. For half-integer
s
(fermions),
(-1)^{2s}=-1
and the wavefunction is antisymmetric. As a result, fermions obey the Pauli exclusion principle (no two identical fermions can occupy the same quantum state), while bosons may occupy the same state.

L.2.2
Completeness

1.

State Space.
(
Axiom 1
) ensures each physical configuration of the system corresponds to a vector in a complex Hilbert space.

2.

Observables.
(
Axiom 2
) stipulates that physical quantities are represented mathematically by self-adjoint operators, allowing us to predict their possible eigenvalues.

3.

Measurement Postulate.
(
Axiom 3
) gives the probabilistic interpretation needed to connect the mathematical formalism to measurable outcomes.

4.

State Collapse.
(
Axiom 4
) specifies how the state changes upon measurement, ensuring consistency with observed discrete measurement results.

5.

Time Evolution.
(
Axiom 5
) describes the deterministic (unitary) evolution of the system in the absence of measurement.

6.

Intrinsic Spin.
(
Axiom 6
) incorporates intrinsic angular momentum, crucial for understanding fine structures, magnetic phenomena, and multi-particle spin states.

7.

Exchange Symmetry and Pauli Principle.
(
Axiom 7
) governs the symmetry or antisymmetry of the wavefunction for identical particles, explaining why fermions cannot share a quantum state while bosons can.

From these seven axioms, one can predict the evolution of quantum systems, compute probabilities of measurement outcomes, and describe a vast range of phenomena, including spin-dependent interactions, statistical effects in multi-particle systems, and the structured formation of matter.

L.2.3
Independence

1.

State Space.
(Axiom 1) cannot be derived from assumptions about measurements or time evolution; it is the foundational structure that underlies the rest.

2.

Observables.
(Axiom 2) cannot be inferred solely from the state space; specifying that observables correspond to self-adjoint operators is an independent assertion about how physical quantities map to operators.

3.

Measurement Postulate.
(Axiom 3) introduces the probabilistic interpretation (Born Rule). It is not implied by the structure of the state space or the form of the Hamiltonian.

4.

State Collapse.
(Axiom 4) is not derivable from continuous time evolution or the existence of observables; it uniquely addresses how the state changes upon a measurement.

5.

Time Evolution.
(Axiom 5) is independent of the measurement process; knowing how measurements yield probabilities does not itself define the unitary dynamics between those measurements.

6.

Intrinsic Spin.
(Axiom 6) is not implied by the previous axioms on its own; it is an additional degree of freedom that arises from the fundamental quantum nature of particles and cannot be derived from orbital degrees of freedom alone.

7.

Exchange Symmetry and Pauli Principle.
(Axiom 7) does not follow from the preceding axioms; it makes a unique statement about the symmetry properties of many-particle wavefunctions, distinguishing bosons from fermions and dictating the Pauli exclusion principle for half-integer spins.

L.3
Transformation

L.3.1
Axiom 1

Goal:

Starting with

S_{0}=\{\,H_{1}\},\hskip 9.24994ptH_{1}:\;(q_{i},p_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},

we aim to arrive at

Q_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\left|\psi\right>\in\mathcal{H},\;\;\langle\psi|\psi\rangle=1.

Rewrite Step 1: From Phase-Space Point to Normalized Wavefunction.

We replace each classical state
(q,p)
by a complex wavefunction
\psi(q)
. Because multiplying
\psi
by a constant does not change physical content, we impose
\int|\psi(q)|^{2}\,dq=1
. This normalization suggests a probability interpretation and naturally requires an inner product

\langle\phi|\psi\rangle=\int\phi^{*}(q)\,\psi(q)\,dq
.

H_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;A_{1}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\psi(q),\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\psi|\psi\rangle=\int|\psi(q)|^{2}\,dq=1.

S_{0}=\{\,H_{1}\}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{1}=\{\,A_{1}^{\prime}\}.

Final Statement (Quantum Axiom 1):

Collecting the above, the state now lives in a complex Hilbert space and is normalized:

Q_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\left|\psi\right>\in\mathcal{H},\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\psi|\psi\rangle=1.

S_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;\{\,Q_{1}\}.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form):

\underbrace{\{\,H_{1}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,A_{1}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{1}\}}_{S_{2}}.

L.3.2
Axiom 2

Goal:

Starting with

S_{0}=\{\,H_{1},\,H_{2},\,Q_{1}\},

where

H_{1}:\;(q_{i},p_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},

H_{2}:\;H(q,p)\;\text{(real-valued Hamiltonian)},

Q_{1}:\;\psi(x)\in\mathcal{H},\;\int|\psi(x)|^{2}\,dx=1,

(
Q_{1}
has already been derived from Hamiltonian axioms) show that real classical observables become self-adjoint operators:

Q_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{A}=\hat{A}^{\dagger}.

Initial Set (Hamilton + Quantum Axiom 1):

\boxed{S_{0}=\{\,H_{1},\,H_{2},\,Q_{1}\}}

Rewrite Step 1: Classical Observables Are Real Functions.

H(q,p)
is real. Assume every classical observable
F(q,p)
is also real-valued:

B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994ptF:\;\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to\mathbb{R}.

S_{0}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{1}=\{\,Q_{1},\,B_{1}\}.

Rewrite Step 2: Replace
F
by an Operator on
\psi(x)
.

Using the Hilbert space from
Q_{1}
, each real
F
is mapped to an operator
\hat{F}
:

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994ptF\;\mapsto\;\hat{F}.

S_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{2}=\{\,Q_{1},\,B_{2}\}.

Rewrite Step 3: Expectation Value in Normalized States.

With
\int|\psi(x)|^{2}\,dx=1
(from
Q_{1}
), define

B_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\hat{F}\rangle=\int\psi^{*}(x)\,[\hat{F}\,\psi](x)\,dx.

S_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{3}=\{\,Q_{1},\,B_{3}\}.

Rewrite Step 4: Self-Adjointness and Real Expectation.

First, for two wavefunctions
\phi,\psi
,

\int\phi^{*}(x)\,[\hat{F}\,\psi](x)\,dx=\int[\hat{F}^{\dagger}\,\phi]^{*}(x)\,\psi(x)\,dx,

defines the
adjoint

\hat{F}^{\dagger}
. Requiring

\langle\hat{F}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
for all
\psi
implies

\int[\hat{F}^{\dagger}\,\psi]^{*}(x)\,\psi(x)\,dx=\langle\hat{F}\rangle=\langle\hat{F}\rangle^{*}=\Bigl(\int\psi^{*}(x)\,[\hat{F}\,\psi](x)\,dx\Bigr)^{*}=\int[\hat{F}\psi]^{*}(x)\,\psi(x)\,dx.

Hence

Q_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{F}=\hat{F}^{\dagger}.

S_{3}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{4}=\{\,Q_{2}\}.

Final Set (Quantum Axiom 2):

\boxed{S_{4}=\Bigl\{Q_{2}:\;\hat{A}=\hat{A}^{\dagger}\Bigr\}.}

Thus, each real classical observable
F(q,p)
is promoted to a self-adjoint operator in quantum mechanics, ensuring real eigenvalues (measurement outcomes).

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form):

\underbrace{\{\,H_{1},H_{2},Q_{1}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{1},B_{1}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{1},B_{2}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{1},B_{3}\}}_{S_{3}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{2}\}}_{S_{4}}.

L.3.3
Axiom 3

Goal:

Having established that (1) states are normalized wavefunctions
\psi\in\mathcal{H}
(
Q_{1}
) and (2) observables are self-adjoint operators
\hat{A}=\hat{A}^{\dagger}
(
Q_{2}
), we now show how the
Born rule
for measurement follows. Specifically, we derive:

Q_{3}:\hskip 9.24994ptP(\alpha_{i})=\bigl|\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle\bigr|^{2},

where
\left|\phi_{i}\right>
are the eigenstates of the self-adjoint operator
\hat{A}
(with eigenvalues
\{\alpha_{i}\}
), and
\psi
is the pre-measurement state.

Initial Set (Quantum Axiom 1, Axiom 2):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{Q_{1},\,Q_{2}\bigr\}}

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle:\hskip 9.24994pt\psi(x)\in\mathcal{H},\;\int|\psi(x)|^{2}\,dx=1,

\displaystyle Q_{2}

\displaystyle:\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{A}=\hat{A}^{\dagger}.

We have already demonstrated how
Q_{1},Q_{2}
follow from Hamiltonian axioms. We next focus on how a measurement of
\hat{A}
yields definite outcomes
\alpha_{i}
with certain probabilities.

Rewrite Step 1: Eigen-Decomposition of Self-Adjoint Operators.

Since
\hat{A}
is self-adjoint (
Q_{2}
), the spectral theorem guarantees it has a complete basis of orthonormal eigenstates
\{\left|\phi_{i}\right>\}
with real eigenvalues
\{\alpha_{i}\}
:

B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{A}\,\left|\phi_{i}\right>=\alpha_{i}\,\left|\phi_{i}\right>,\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\phi_{i}\mid\phi_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}.

In functional analysis, every self-adjoint operator on a Hilbert space admits such an eigen-decomposition. This
directly follows
from
Q_{2}
.

S_{0}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{1}=\{\;B_{1}\}.

Rewrite Step 2: Introduce Projectors Onto Each Eigenstate.

Define the projection operator
\hat{P}_{i}:=\left|\phi_{i}\right>\!\left\langle\phi_{i}\right|
. This operator extracts the component of any state
\left|\psi\right>
in the direction of
\left|\phi_{i}\right>
:

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{P}_{i}\,\left|\psi\right>=\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle\;\left|\phi_{i}\right>.

Reasoning:
Projection operators arise naturally once we accept a basis of eigenstates. They map
\left|\psi\right>
to its “shadow” along
\left|\phi_{i}\right>
. Hence, introducing
\hat{P}_{i}
is justified by the
linearity of quantum theory
(which is embedded in
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
) and the orthonormality of
\left|\phi_{i}\right>
.

S_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{2}=\{\;B_{1},B_{2}\}.

Rewrite Step 3: Probability as the Norm of the Projected State.

We connect measurement outcomes to the squared amplitude of
\psi
along each eigenvector
\phi_{i}
. Specifically,

B_{3}:\hskip 9.24994ptP(\alpha_{i})=\bigl\|\hat{P}_{i}\,\left|\psi\right>\bigr\|^{2}=\bigl|\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle\bigr|^{2}.

Reasoning:

•

The probabilistic interpretation stems from
Q_{1}
’s normalization condition (
\int|\psi|^{2}=1
).

•

Once we accept that outcomes must be linked to the eigenstates of
\hat{A}
, the simplest and most consistent definition of
P(\alpha_{i})
is the squared norm of the projection.

S_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{3}=\{\,B_{3}\}.

Final Set (Quantum Axiom 3, Born Rule):

Hence, the probability that measuring
\hat{A}
yields the eigenvalue
\alpha_{i}
is:

\boxed{Q_{3}:\hskip 9.24994ptP(\alpha_{i})=\bigl|\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle\bigr|^{2}.}

S_{3}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{4}=\{\,Q_{3}\}.

This result completes the connection between self-adjoint operators and observable measurement outcomes in quantum theory. The inner product
\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle
naturally encodes the “overlap” between the current state
\psi
and the eigenstate
\phi_{i}
, thus defining a probability in a normalized Hilbert space.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form):

\underbrace{\{\,Q_{1},\,Q_{2}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\;B_{1}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\;B_{1},B_{2}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,B_{3}\}}_{S_{3}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{3}\}}_{S_{4}}.

L.3.4
Axiom 4

Goal:

Having established states as wavefunctions
\psi
(
Q_{1}
), observables as self-adjoint operators
\hat{A}
(
Q_{2}
), and the Born rule for measurement outcomes (
Q_{3}
), we now introduce the
state collapse
axiom:

Q_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Upon measuring }\hat{A}\text{ and obtaining }\alpha_{i},\;\psi\;\longmapsto\;\phi_{i},

where
\left|\phi_{i}\right>
is the corresponding eigenstate of
\hat{A}
. This postulate tells us how the wavefunction changes
immediately
after a definite measurement outcome.

Initial Set (Quantum Axioms 1, 2, 3):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,Q_{1},\,Q_{2},\,Q_{3}\bigr\}}

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle:\psi(x)\;\text{with }\int|\psi|^{2}=1,\hskip 9.24994ptQ_{2}

\displaystyle:\hat{A}=\hat{A}^{\dagger},\hskip 9.24994ptQ_{3}

\displaystyle:P(\alpha_{i})=|\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle|^{2}.

Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}
can be derived from Hamiltonian axioms as specified in previous sections. Having a self-adjoint operator
\hat{A}
with eigenstates
\{\phi_{i}\}
and the probability rule for observing eigenvalue
\alpha_{i}
, we now specify the post-measurement state.

Rewrite Step 1: Identify the Projected State.

From
Q_{3}
, measuring
\hat{A}
yields the result
\alpha_{i}
with probability
\|\hat{P}_{i}\psi\|^{2}
, where
\hat{P}_{i}=\left|\phi_{i}\right>\!\left\langle\phi_{i}\right|
. After the event
\alpha_{i}
has been observed, we make a physical assumption that the state of the system will not change. By
Q_{3}
(since eigenstates form a basis) the probability of other outcomes being zero means the state is described by the particular projection:

B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\psi\;\longmapsto\;\hat{P}_{i}\,\psi=\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle\,\phi_{i}.

We assume the state is now entirely in the subspace corresponding to
\alpha_{i}
.

S_{0}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{1}=\{\,B_{1}\}.

Rewrite Step 2: Normalize the Projected State.

To preserve the interpretation
\int|\psi(x)|^{2}\,dx=1
, we must normalize
\hat{P}_{i}\,\psi
. Thus,

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\psi\;\longmapsto\;\frac{\hat{P}_{i}\,\psi}{\|\hat{P}_{i}\,\psi\|}=\frac{\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle}{\sqrt{|\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle|^{2}}}\;\phi_{i}=\phi_{i}.

Hence immediately after measuring
\alpha_{i}
, the wavefunction collapses to
\left|\phi_{i}\right>
.

S_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{2}=\{\,Q_{4}\}.

Final Set (Quantum Axiom 4, State Collapse):

\boxed{Q_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{If measuring }\hat{A}\text{ yields }\alpha_{i},\;\psi\longmapsto\phi_{i}.}

Thus, upon obtaining a specific eigenvalue
\alpha_{i}
, the post-measurement wavefunction becomes the corresponding eigenstate
\left|\phi_{i}\right>
.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form):

\underbrace{\{\,Q_{1},\,Q_{2},\,Q_{3}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,B_{1}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{4}\}}_{S_{2}}

L.3.5
Axiom 5

Goal:

Starting from (axioms
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}
have been derived from Hamiltonian axioms as shown above).

S_{0}=\{\,H_{1},\,H_{2},\,Q_{1},\,Q_{2},\,Q_{3}\},

where

\displaystyle H_{1}

\displaystyle:(q_{i},p_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},

\displaystyle H_{2}

\displaystyle:H(q,p)\;\text{denotes total energy (Hamiltonian)},

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle:\psi(x)\in\mathcal{H},\;\int|\psi(x)|^{2}\,dx=1,

\displaystyle Q_{2}

\displaystyle:\hat{A}=\hat{A}^{\dagger},

\displaystyle Q_{3}

\displaystyle:P(\alpha_{i})=\bigl|\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle\bigr|^{2},

we establish the Schrödinger equation:

Q_{5}:\hskip 9.24994pti\,\hbar\,\frac{d}{dt}\,\psi(t)=\hat{H}\,\psi(t),

where
\hat{H}
is the self-adjoint operator corresponding to the classical Hamiltonian
H
.

Initial Set (Hamilton + Quantum Axioms 1–4):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,H_{1},\,H_{2},\,Q_{1},\,Q_{2},\,Q_{3},\,Q_{4}\bigr\}}

Rewrite Step 1: Poisson Bracket from Hamiltonian Mechanics.

Given a classical observable
F(q,p)
, its total time derivative in Hamiltonian theory is

\frac{dF}{dt}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl(\frac{\partial F}{\partial q_{i}}\,\dot{q}_{i}+\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{i}}\,\dot{p}_{i}\Bigr).

Using Hamilton’s equations

\dot{q}_{i}=\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}}
,

\dot{p}_{i}=-\frac{\partial H}{\partial q_{i}}
,
we define the Poisson bracket:

B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\{F,H\}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl(\frac{\partial F}{\partial q_{i}}\,\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}}-\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{i}}\,\frac{\partial H}{\partial q_{i}}\Bigr),\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{dF}{dt}=\{F,H\}.

S_{0}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{1}=\{\,B_{1},Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}.

Rewrite Step 2: From Poisson Bracket to the “Relativistic” Quantum Commutator.

Translating into quantum form, we replace

\{F,H\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longmapsto\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{1}{i\hbar}\,[\,\hat{F},\,\hat{H}\,],

with
[\hat{F},\hat{H}]=\hat{F}\hat{H}-\hat{H}\hat{F}
. This suggests an operator relation for the time derivative of the expectation value:

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pti\,\hbar\,\frac{d}{dt}\,\bigl\langle\hat{F}\bigr\rangle=\Bigl\langle\,\bigl[\hat{F},\,\hat{H}\bigr]\Bigr\rangle.

S_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{2}=\{\,B_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}.

Rewrite Step 3: Expand Both Sides to Deduce the Schrödinger Equation.

Consider

i\,\hbar\,\frac{d}{dt}\,\int\psi^{*}(x)\,\hat{F}\,\psi(x)\,dx.

By the product rule in the Schrödinger picture,

i\,\hbar\frac{d}{dt}\int\psi^{*}(x)\,\hat{F}\,\psi(x)\,dx=\int\Bigl(i\hbar\,\frac{d\psi^{*}(x)}{dt}\Bigr)\hat{F}\,\psi(x)\,dx+\int\psi^{*}(x)\,\hat{F}\,\Bigl(i\hbar\,\frac{d\psi(x)}{dt}\Bigr)dx.

Meanwhile,

\bigl\langle[\hat{F},\,\hat{H}]\bigr\rangle=\int\psi^{*}(x)\,\Bigl(\hat{F}\,\hat{H}-\hat{H}\,\hat{F}\Bigr)\,\psi(x)\,dx=-\int(\hat{H}\,\psi)^{*}(x)\,\hat{F}\,\psi(x)\,dx+\int\psi^{*}(x)\,\hat{F}\,\bigr(\hat{H}\,\psi(x)\,\bigl)dx.

Where we have used the fact that
\hat{H}
is self-adjoint (energy operator). Comparing these terms one arrives at

Q_{5}:\hskip 9.24994pti\hbar\,\frac{d}{dt}\,\psi(x,t)=\hat{H}\,\psi(x,t).

S_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{3}=\{\,Q_{5}\}.

Final Set (Quantum Axiom 5, Time-Dependent Schrödinger Equation):

Q_{5}:\hskip 9.24994pti\,\hbar\,\frac{d}{dt}\,\psi(t)=\hat{H}\,\psi(t).

Thus the Hamiltonian in quantum mechanics drives the wavefunction’s time evolution.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form):

\underbrace{\bigl\{\,H_{1},H_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\bigr\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,B_{1},Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,B_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{5}\}}_{S_{3}}.

L.3.6
Axiom 6

Goal:

Having already established that

S_{0}=\{\,Q_{1},\,Q_{2},\,Q_{3}\},

where

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle:\psi(x)\in\mathcal{H},\;\int|\psi(x)|^{2}\,dx=1,

\displaystyle Q_{2}

\displaystyle:\hat{A}=\hat{A}^{\dagger},

\displaystyle Q_{3}

\displaystyle:P(\alpha_{i})=\bigl|\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle\bigr|^{2},

we now, using experimental evidence, introduce the intrinsic
spin
degree of freedom in quantum mechanics:

Q_{6}:\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{2}\,\left|\psi\right>=\hbar^{2}\,s(s+1)\,\left|\psi\right>,\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{S}_{z}\,\left|\psi\right>=\hbar\,m_{s}\,\left|\psi\right>,

with
m_{s}\in\{-s,-s+1,\ldots,s\}
and
s\in\{0,\tfrac{1}{2},1,\tfrac{3}{2},\ldots\}
.

Initial Set (Quantum Axioms 1–3):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,Q_{1},\,Q_{2},\,Q_{3}\bigr\}}

We have a Hilbert space of states, observables as self-adjoint operators, and the Born rule for measurement probabilities.

Rewrite Step 1: Experimental Evidence for an Additional Degree of Freedom.

Phenomena like the Stern-Gerlach experiment show discrete outcomes not explained by orbital angular momentum alone. This implies a new, irreducible quantum number:

B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Wavefunction has ``intrinsic spin'' beyond }(x,y,z).

S_{0}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{1}=\{\,B_{1},Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}.

Rewrite Step 2: Introduce the Spin Operator
\hat{\mathbf{S}}
.

By
Q_{2}
, any observable is a self-adjoint operator. We define
\hat{\mathbf{S}}
satisfying the usual angular momentum algebra but irreducible with half-integer possibilities:

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{\mathbf{S}}=\bigl(\hat{S}_{x},\hat{S}_{y},\hat{S}_{z}\bigr),\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{\dagger}=\hat{\mathbf{S}}.

S_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{2}=\{\,B_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}.

Rewrite Step 3: Measurement of Spin via the Born Rule.

Using
Q_{3}
, measuring
\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{2}
or
\hat{S}_{z}
yields discrete eigenvalues. We use the analogy of spin with angular momentum. After solving the corresponding differential equations (which lead to spherical Harmonics), the eigenvalues of the total angular momentum operator
\hat{L}^{2}
and
z
-component of the angular momentum
\hat{L}_{z}
turn out to be
\hbar^{2}l(l+1)
and
\hbar m_{l}\;(-l\leq m_{l}\leq l)
, respectively. Analogously,

Q_{6}:\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{2}\,\left|\psi\right>=\hbar^{2}\,s(s+1)\,\left|\psi\right>,\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{S}_{z}\,\left|\psi\right>=\hbar\,m_{s}\,\left|\psi\right>,

with
m_{s}\in\{-s,\dots,s\}
and
s
possibly half-integer. The Born rule gives probabilities
|\langle\phi_{i}|\psi\rangle|^{2}
for each outcome.

S_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{3}=\{\,Q_{6}\}.

Final Set (Quantum Axiom 6, Intrinsic Spin):

\boxed{Q_{6}:\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{2}\,\left|\psi\right>=\hbar^{2}\,s(s+1)\,\left|\psi\right>,\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{S}_{z}\,\left|\psi\right>=\hbar\,m_{s}\,\left|\psi\right>,}

where
s
can be integer or half-integer and
m_{s}
runs from
-s
to
+s
in unit steps.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form):

\underbrace{\{\,Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,B_{1},Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,B_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{6}\}}_{S_{3}}

L.3.7
Axiom 7

Goal:

Starting with

S_{0}=\{\,Q_{1},\,Q_{3},\,Q_{6}\},

(derived from Hamiltonian axioms) where

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle:\psi(x)\in\mathcal{H},\;\int|\psi(x)|^{2}\,dx=1,

\displaystyle Q_{3}

\displaystyle:P(\alpha_{i})=\bigl|\langle\phi_{i}\mid\psi\rangle\bigr|^{2},

\displaystyle Q_{6}

\displaystyle:\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{2}\,\left|\psi\right>=\hbar^{2}\,s(s+1)\,\left|\psi\right>,\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{S}_{z}\,\left|\psi\right>=\hbar\,m_{s}\,\left|\psi\right>,

and using experimental evidence we introduce the
exchange symmetry
for identical particles and the resulting Pauli principle:

Q_{7}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Psi(\dots,\mathbf{r}_{i},\sigma_{i},\dots,\mathbf{r}_{j},\sigma_{j},\dots)=(-1)^{2s}\,\Psi(\dots,\mathbf{r}_{j},\sigma_{j},\dots,\mathbf{r}_{i},\sigma_{i},\dots).

For half-integers (
2s
odd), the wavefunction is antisymmetric (Pauli exclusion). For integers (
2s
even), it is symmetric (bosons can occupy the same state).

Initial Set (Basic Quantum + Spin):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,Q_{1},\,Q_{3},\,Q_{6}\bigr\}}

We have the notion of normalized states, the Born rule for probabilities, and intrinsic spin with spin quantum number
s
.

Rewrite Step 1: Identical Particles Are Indistinguishable.

Experimentally, swapping two identical particles does not produce a physically new configuration. The total wavefunction
\Psi(\mathbf{r}_{1},\sigma_{1};\dots,\mathbf{r}_{N},\sigma_{N})
must reflect this indistinguishability:

B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Exchanging any two identical particles yields the same physical state}.

S_{0}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{1}=\{\,Q_{1},Q_{3},Q_{6},B_{1}\}.

Rewrite Step 2: Introduce Exchange Operator.

Define an operator
\hat{P}_{ij}
that permutes (exchanges) particles
i
and
j
. Since the system is identical-particle-based,

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{P}_{ij}\,\Psi(\dots,\mathbf{r}_{i},\sigma_{i},\dots,\mathbf{r}_{j},\sigma_{j},\dots)=\Psi(\dots,\mathbf{r}_{j},\sigma_{j},\dots,\mathbf{r}_{i},\sigma_{i},\dots).

Measured probabilities must be invariant under
\hat{P}_{ij}
because there is no way to distinguish which particle is which.

S_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{2}=\{\,Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},B_{2}\}.

Rewrite Step 3: Relate Exchange to Spin.

In non-relativistic quantum theory, one finds that
\hat{P}_{ij}^{2}=1
(exchanging twice returns the original configuration), so the eigenvalues of
\hat{P}_{ij}
are
\pm 1
. For spin-
s
particles, exchanging two such particles yields a factor of
(-1)^{2s}
. Symbolically,

B_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Psi\,\xrightarrow{\;\hat{P}_{ij}\;}\,(-1)^{2s}\,\Psi.

If
s
is half-integer,
\Psi
is antisymmetric (fermionic), and if
s
is integer,
\Psi
is symmetric (bosonic).

S_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{3}=\{\,B_{3}\}.

Final Set (Quantum Axiom 7, Exchange Symmetry):

Q_{7}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Psi(\dots,\mathbf{r}_{i},\sigma_{i},\dots,\mathbf{r}_{j},\sigma_{j},\dots)=(-1)^{2s}\,\Psi(\dots,\mathbf{r}_{j},\sigma_{j},\dots,\mathbf{r}_{i},\sigma_{i},\dots).

For
s=\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2},\dots
(fermions), the wavefunction is antisymmetric, and no two fermions can occupy the same quantum state (Pauli principle). For
s=0,1,2,\dots
(bosons), it is symmetric.

S_{3}\;\longrightarrow\;S_{4}=\{\,Q_{7}\}.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form):

\underbrace{\{\,Q_{1},Q_{3},Q_{6}\}}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},B_{1}\}}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},B_{2}\}}_{S_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,B_{3}\}}_{S_{3}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{\{\,Q_{7}\}}_{S_{4}}.

Appendix M
From Classical Genetics to Molecular/Genomic Biology

M.1
Classical Genetics

Classical Genetics is the study of how traits are transmitted from parents to offspring, focusing on the foundational principles that govern inheritance.
Early work by Gregor Mendel introduced key laws describing how discrete units of heredity (genes) segregate and assort during gamete formation.
Subsequent findings located these genes on chromosomes, connecting their physical behavior during meiosis to observable inheritance patterns in organisms.

M.1.1
Axioms

1.

Law of Segregation

\forall\,O\in\mathrm{Diploid},\ \forall\,G=(A_{1},A_{2}):\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{aligned} &A_{1}\text{ and }A_{2}\text{ separate during gamete}\\
&\text{formation, each gamete inherits }\\
&\text{exactly one }A_{i}.\end{aligned}

2.

Law of Independent Assortment

\forall\,(G_{1},G_{2})\text{ on separate chromosomes}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{segregation of }G_{1})\perp(\text{segregation of }G_{2}).

3.

Law of Dominance

\forall\,(\text{genotype }Aa):\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{phenotype of }A)>(\text{phenotype of }a).

4.

Chromosomal Inheritance

\forall\,G:\ \bigl(G\subseteq(\text{chromosomes})\bigr)\wedge\bigl(\text{chromosomes segregate in meiosis}\bigr).

M.1.2
Completeness

1.

Law of Segregation.
(
Axiom 1
) covers how alleles for a single gene partition into gametes within a diploid organism.

2.

Independent Assortment & Dominance.

Axiom 2
and
Axiom 3
ensure that multiple genes (on separate chromosomes) follow independent segregation patterns, and also define how phenotypes manifest when one allele is dominant over another.

3.

Chromosomal Inheritance.
(
Axiom 4
) encapsulates the physical basis of these laws by placing genes on chromosomes, unifying the segregation and assortment mechanisms with observable meiotic behavior.

From these four, one can construct Punnett squares and describe monohybrid or dihybrid crosses, track inheritance patterns, and predict phenotypic ratios in progeny.
They suffice to explain classical inheritance in many organisms, forming the cornerstone of genetics before the molecular details of DNA were elucidated.

M.1.3
Independence

1.

Law of Segregation.
cannot be deduced from independent assortment or dominance alone, nor from chromosomal inheritance without explicitly stating that each gamete receives exactly one allele per gene.

2.

Law of Independent Assortment.
cannot be derived from segregation, dominance, or chromosomal inheritance alone, since it specifically addresses the independence of allele pairs on separate chromosomes.

3.

Law of Dominance.
is not derivable from segregation or independent assortment, which only address allele partitioning, not how one allele’s expression can mask another’s.

4.

Chromosomal Inheritance.
does not follow from any combination of segregation, independent assortment, or dominance. These three axioms describe the distribution of alleles and their expression, but do not guarantee a physical location (chromosomes) for genes without stating this principle separately.

M.2
Molecular/Genomic Biology

Molecular/Genomic Biology focuses on the structure and function of genetic
material at the molecular level. It explains how DNA is organized into
genes, how it is replicated and transcribed into RNA, and how RNA is
translated into proteins. Regulatory mechanisms further control these
processes, allowing cells to respond and adapt to their environments.

M.2.1
Axioms

1.

Double Helix & Replication

\forall\,D:\ D=(S_{1},S_{2})\text{ - complementary strands}\ \wedge\ \Bigl[(\forall\,i)\,S_{i}\to(\text{template for new }S^{\prime}_{i})\Bigr].

(DNA is formed by two complementary strands
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
.
Each strand serves as a template in replication.)

2.

Central Dogma

\forall\,G\subseteq D:\ G\to(\text{transcription }\to\mathrm{mRNA})\to(\text{translation }\to\mathrm{polypeptide}).

(A gene
G
in DNA is transcribed into messenger RNA, which is
then translated into a polypeptide chain.)

3.

Genetic Code Universality

\forall\,(\mathrm{codon}\ c):\hskip 9.24994ptc\mapsto\mathrm{amino\ acid}\ a,

(with minor exceptions, the same codons specify identical amino acids
across most organisms.)

4.

Regulation of Gene Expression

\forall\,(\text{gene }g):\ \text{expression}(g)=f(\mathrm{TFs},\mathrm{promoters},\mathrm{enhancers},\mathrm{epigenetics}).

(Each gene’s expression level depends on transcription factors, promoter
sequences, enhancer elements, and epigenetic modifications.)

M.2.2
Completeness

1.

Double Helix & Replication.
(
Axiom 1
) describes the
physical structure of DNA and its templated replication mechanism.

2.

Central Dogma & Code.

Axiom 2
and
Axiom 3
