capture the flow of genetic information (DNA to RNA to protein) and the
universal mapping from codons to amino acids.

3.

Regulation.
(
Axiom 4
) incorporates the control systems
that modulate gene expression, making the informational framework
responsive to cellular and environmental states.

These four axioms provide a foundation for describing DNA replication,
transcription, translation, and regulation. They enable us to model both
the static sequence aspects of genetic information and its dynamic
expression patterns in cells.

M.2.3
Independence

1.

Double Helix & Replication.
cannot be inferred from
the Central Dogma or the Genetic Code alone; they address the flow of
information but not the physical structure enabling replication.

2.

Central Dogma.
is not implied by the double-helix structure or
the universality of codons; it specifically prescribes transcription
and translation as core processes.

3.

Genetic Code Universality.
does not follow from the Central
Dogma or replication; it concerns the specific mappings from codons
to amino acids.

4.

Regulation of Gene Expression.
cannot be deduced from any
combination of the preceding axioms alone, since it describes how the
basic processes are modulated and controlled, rather than how they
fundamentally operate in sequence.

M.3
Transformations

M.3.1
Axiom 1

Goal:
Transform a subset of Classical Genetics axioms

\bigl\{\,A_{1},\;A_{4}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{aligned} A_{1}&:\text{Law of Segregation},\\
A_{4}&:\text{Chromosomal Inheritance},\end{aligned}

into the
Molecular/Genomic Biology
statement:

W_{1}:\forall\,D:\ D=(S_{1},S_{2})\text{ - complementary strands}\ \wedge\ \Bigl[(\forall\,i)\,S_{i}\to(\text{template for }S^{\prime}_{i})\Bigr].

This final statement
W_{1}
is the
Double Helix & Replication
axiom, describing DNA as two complementary strands
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
that each serve as a template for replication.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Genetics, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,A_{1},\;A_{4}\bigr\}}

where

A_{1}\;(\text{Law of Segregation}):\;\forall\,O\in\mathrm{Diploid},\ \forall\,G=(A_{1},A_{2}):\begin{aligned} &A_{1}\text{ and }A_{2}\text{ separate during gamete formation,}\\
&\text{each gamete inherits exactly one }A_{i},\end{aligned}

A_{4}\;(\text{Chromosomal Inheritance}):\hskip 9.24994ptG\subseteq(\text{chromosomes})\wedge(\text{chromosomes segregate in meiosis}).

Rewrite Step 1: Interpret “Segregation” (
A_{1}
)
as the Need for Replicable Carriers.

A_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{1},

where

B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{each allele }A_{i}\text{ must be physically copied to yield})\ (\text{distinct gametes with }A_{i}).

Symbolically, we recast the pure
segregation
principle
as a requirement that some
replicable substrate

carries each allele into separate gametes.
Thus, the act of “separation” implies there must be a duplicative mechanism
to ensure each gamete has
one copy
.

S_{0}=\{\,A_{1},\,A_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,B_{1},\,A_{4}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\bigl\{\,B_{1},\;A_{4}\bigr\}}

where

B_{1}:\ \text{Law of Segregation rephrased as a need for a replicable unit},\hskip 9.24994ptA_{4}:\ \text{genes reside on chromosomes that segregate in meiosis}.

Rewrite Step 2:
Combine Chromosomal Inheritance (
A_{4}
) with the Replication Requirement (
B_{1}
).

\{\,B_{1},\,A_{4}\}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{2},

where

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{the physical carriers of genes must self-duplicate}),\ \text{since each chromosome is inherited in a single copy per gamete}.

That is, if chromosomes are the
physical
location for alleles (
A_{4}
),
and each allele must
replicate
so gametes inherit one copy (
B_{1}
),
then chromosomes themselves must duplicate prior to gamete formation.

S_{1}=\{\,B_{1},\,A_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,B_{2}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\bigl\{\,B_{2}\bigr\}}

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{the chromosome (carrier of alleles) is capable of templated self-duplication.}

Rewrite Step 3:
Identify the Molecular Basis of Self-Duplication
as a Double-Stranded Structure.

Finally, we specify that

the replicable chromosome

is realized by a
double-stranded
molecule
D=(S_{1},S_{2})
,
where each strand acts as a template for a new complementary strand (
S^{\prime}_{1},S^{\prime}_{2}
).
Symbolically:

B_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;W_{1},

where

W_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,D:\ D=(S_{1},S_{2})\text{ - complementary strands}\ \wedge\ \Bigl[(\forall\,i)\,S_{i}\to(\text{template for }S^{\prime}_{i})\Bigr].

In other words, the
physical duplicative carrier
is a double-helix molecule
enabling semiconservative replication.

S_{2}=\{\,B_{2}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\,W_{1}\}.

Final Set (Molecular/Genomic Axiom 1: Double Helix & Replication):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{W_{1}:\ \forall\,D=(S_{1},S_{2})\text{ - complementary strands}\ \wedge\ (\forall\,i)\,S_{i}\to(\text{template for new }S^{\prime}_{i})\Bigr\}.}

This is precisely the
Double Helix & Replication
axiom:
DNA consists of two complementary strands, each serving as a template for replication,
fulfilling the requirement from classical segregation that
each gamete
inherits exactly one copy of every allele.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,A_{1},\,A_{4}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{1},\,A_{4}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{2}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,W_{1}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Each rewrite step shows how the classical notion of “alleles segregating on chromosomes”
inevitably implies a
molecular duplicative structure
for the hereditary material,
leading to the Double Helix & Replication axiom.

M.3.2
Axiom 2

Goal:
Transform a subset of Classical Genetics axioms

\bigl\{\,A_{3},\;A_{4}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{aligned} A_{3}&:\text{Law of Dominance},\\
A_{4}&:\text{Chromosomal Inheritance},\end{aligned}

into the
Molecular/Genomic Biology
statement:

W_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,G\subseteq D:\ G\;\longrightarrow\;(\mathrm{transcription}\,\to\,\mathrm{mRNA})\;\longrightarrow\;(\mathrm{translation}\,\to\,\mathrm{polypeptide}),

which is the
Central Dogma
axiom, stating that each gene
G
in the DNA (
D
) is transcribed into an RNA intermediate and then translated into a polypeptide.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Genetics, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,A_{3},\;A_{4}\bigr\}}

where

A_{3}\;(\text{Law of Dominance}):\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{phenotype of }A)>(\text{phenotype of }a),

A_{4}\;(\text{Chromosomal Inheritance}):\hskip 9.24994ptG\subseteq(\text{chromosomes}),\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{chromosomes segregate in meiosis}).

Here,
A_{3}
says a dominant allele
A
“overrides” the recessive one
a
in the phenotype, and
A_{4}
asserts that genes
G
reside on chromosomes, physically inherited.

Rewrite Step 1: Recast Dominance (
A_{3}
) as an “Expressible Product” Principle.

A_{3}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{3},

where

B_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{the `dominant' allele }A\text{ has a functional output}),\;(\text{the `recessive' }a\text{ is overshadowed in phenotype}).

Symbolically, we interpret the
dominant
allele’s greater phenotypic effect as arising from a more potent or more readily produced
molecular product
, while the recessive allele’s product is masked or not visibly manifested to the same degree.

S_{0}=\{\,A_{3},\,A_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,B_{3},\,A_{4}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\,B_{3},\;A_{4}\}}

where

B_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{dominant allele produces a functional product}),\hskip 9.24994ptA_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{gene }G\subseteq\text{chromosome, which is inherited}).

Rewrite Step 2: Combine Chromosomal Inheritance (
A_{4}
) with
“Functional Output” Concept (
B_{3}
).

\{\,B_{3},\,A_{4}\}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{4},

where

B_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{a gene }G\text{ on the chromosome encodes a}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\text{heritable, functional output manifesting the phenotype}).

That is, we merge the idea that genes
physically reside
on chromosomes (
A_{4}
) with the idea that
dominant alleles
produce a detectably different (often more potent) molecular/phenotypic outcome (
B_{3}
).
Hence each gene must carry instructions that
yield
that functional output.

S_{1}=\{\,B_{3},\,A_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,B_{4}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\bigl\{\,B_{4}\bigr\}}

B_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{the gene }G\text{ (on a chromosome) encodes some transmissible,}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\text{functional output that determines phenotype}.

Rewrite Step 3: Identify the Mechanism for “Functional Output” as a
Gene
\to
RNA
\to
Protein Sequence.

We now specify how the
functional output
is produced at the molecular level.
If the gene
G
is
physically
a DNA segment, it must generate a
polypeptide product
(or functional RNA) via a two-step process: transcription and translation.

Symbolically:

B_{4}\;\longrightarrow\;W_{2},

where

W_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,G\subseteq D:\ \Bigl[G\;\longrightarrow\;(\mathrm{transcription}\;\to\;\mathrm{mRNA})\;\longrightarrow\;(\mathrm{translation}\;\to\;\mathrm{polypeptide})\Bigr].

This final statement is precisely the
Central Dogma
axiom:
the
information
in a gene is expressed through an RNA intermediate into a protein, explaining how a “dominant” version can overshadow a “recessive” one via more potent or differently regulated polypeptide output.

S_{2}=\{\,B_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\,W_{2}\}.

Final Set (Molecular/Genomic Axiom 2: Central Dogma):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{W_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,G\subseteq D:\;(\,G\to(\mathrm{transcription}\to\mathrm{mRNA})\to(\mathrm{translation}\to\mathrm{polypeptide}))\Bigr\}.}

Thus, from the classical notion of
dominance
and the physical placement of genes on chromosomes, one deduces that a gene encodes a functional molecular output—most commonly via an RNA intermediate, followed by protein synthesis—consistent with the
Central Dogma
of molecular biology.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,A_{3},\,A_{4}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{3},\,A_{4}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{4}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,W_{2}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Each rewrite step shows how the basic classical-genetic principle of an allele’s phenotypic
dominance
becomes reinterpreted at the molecular level as a
gene
that is
transcribed
and

M.3.3

Axiom 3

Goal:
Transform a subset of Classical Genetics axioms

\bigl\{\,A_{2},\;A_{4}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{aligned} A_{2}&:\text{Law of Independent Assortment},\\
A_{4}&:\text{Chromosomal Inheritance},\end{aligned}

into the
Molecular/Genomic Biology
statement:

W_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,(\mathrm{codon}\ c):\hskip 9.24994ptc\mapsto\mathrm{amino\ acid}\ a,

which is the
Genetic Code Universality
axiom. It asserts that a three-nucleotide codon
c
is mapped to the same amino acid
a
(with minor exceptions) across most organisms.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Genetics, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,A_{2},\;A_{4}\bigr\}}

where

A_{2}\;(\text{Law of Independent Assortment}):\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{segregation of }G_{1})\;\perp\;(\text{segregation of }G_{2})\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for genes on separate chromosomes},

A_{4}\;(\text{Chromosomal Inheritance}):\hskip 9.24994ptG\subseteq(\text{chromosomes}),\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{chromosomes segregate in meiosis}).

Here,
A_{2}
asserts that different genes assort independently when located on separate chromosomes;
A_{4}
asserts that any gene
G
physically resides on a chromosome, which is inherited as a unit.

Rewrite Step 1: Interpret Independent Assortment (
A_{2}
) as Modular, Re-combinable Information.

A_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{2},

where

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{each gene }G_{i}\text{ has an autonomous, `modular'}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\text{sequence that can be assorted and combined with others}).

Symbolically, we reframe
independent assortment
to mean each gene’s hereditary information is somewhat
self-contained
, allowing new combinations to arise without altering the coding capacity of any other gene.

S_{0}=\{\,A_{2},\,A_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,B_{2},\,A_{4}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\bigl\{\,B_{2},\;A_{4}\bigr\}}

where

B_{2}:\ \text{genes are modular units of information that can be}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\text{independently assorted and combined in progeny},

A_{4}:\ \text{genes reside on chromosomes, which segregate in meiosis}.

Rewrite Step 2: Combine Chromosomal Inheritance (
A_{4}
)
with “Modular Gene” (
B_{2}
) to Imply a Shared Reading Mechanism.

\{\,B_{2},\,A_{4}\}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{3},

where

B_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{any gene on a chromosome must be `read'}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\text{by a common cellular system to yield a functional product}).

Since each gene is a modular unit and yet all genes lie on chromosomes (in the same cellular environment), it follows that
one
decoding system is shared by all these modular units—otherwise, independent assortment would fail to preserve coherent trait expression across diverse crosses.

S_{1}=\{\,B_{2},\,A_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,B_{3}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,B_{3}\}}

B_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{a unified decoding or reading apparatus must exist,}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\text{applicable to each independently assorted gene on chromosomes}.

Rewrite Step 3: Specify that “Unified Decoding” is the Universal
Codon-to-Amino-Acid Mapping.

Finally, we formalize how a
common
decoding system applies to any gene’s coding region. The essential statement is that a triple-nucleotide sequence (
codon
) is mapped to a specific amino acid
universally
, ensuring that each gene, no matter its chromosomal locus or combination, is read by the
same
translation code.

Symbolically:

B_{3}\;\longrightarrow\;W_{3},

where

W_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall(\mathrm{codon}\ c):\ c\mapsto\mathrm{amino\ acid}\ a.

This final statement is the
Genetic Code Universality
axiom, guaranteeing consistent protein-coding across virtually all organisms (with minor exceptions).

S_{2}=\{\,B_{3}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\,W_{3}\}.

Final Set (Molecular/Genomic Axiom 3: Genetic Code Universality):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{W_{3}:\ \forall\,(\mathrm{codon}\ c):\hskip 9.24994ptc\mapsto\mathrm{amino\ acid}\ a\Bigr\}.}

Thus, from the classical idea that
genes are independently assorted
(
A_{2}
) and
reside on chromosomes
(
A_{4}
), we conclude there must be a
universal system
to interpret each gene’s information. At the molecular level, that system is the universal genetic code mapping codons to amino acids.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,A_{2},\,A_{4}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{2},\,A_{4}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{3}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,W_{3}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Each rewrite step shows how the independent, modular nature of genes and their chromosomal inheritance jointly imply a
common
decoding mechanism for all coding sequences, yielding the
Genetic Code Universality
axiom in molecular/genomic biology.

M.3.4

Axiom 4

Goal:
Transform a subset of Classical Genetics axioms

\bigl\{\,A_{1},\,A_{2},\,A_{3}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{aligned} A_{1}&:\text{Law of Segregation},\\
A_{2}&:\text{Law of Independent Assortment},\\
A_{3}&:\text{Law of Dominance},\end{aligned}

into the
Molecular/Genomic Biology
statement:

W_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,(\text{gene }g):\ \text{expression}(g)=f(\mathrm{TFs},\mathrm{promoters},\mathrm{enhancers},\mathrm{epigenetics}),

which is the
Regulation of Gene Expression
axiom. It says that a gene’s expression level depends on various regulatory factors (transcription factors, promoters, enhancers, and epigenetic marks), thereby allowing variable phenotypic outcomes even for the same genotype.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Genetics, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{\,A_{1},\,A_{2},\,A_{3}\bigr\}}

where

A_{1}\;(\text{Segregation}):\hskip 9.24994ptA_{1}\text{ and }A_{2}\text{ separate in gamete formation},

A_{2}\;(\text{Independent Assortment}):\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{segregation of }G_{1})\perp(\text{segregation of }G_{2})\text{ for distinct loci on different chromosomes},

A_{3}\;(\text{Dominance}):\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{phenotype of }A)\;>\;(\text{phenotype of }a).

Collectively, these define how alleles segregate and assort, with certain alleles having a “dominant” phenotypic influence.

Rewrite Step 1: From Classical Phenotypic Patterns to
Varying Expression Levels.

\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{1},

where

B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Classical observations show that even with fixed alleles,}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\text{phenotypes can vary}

(e.g. incomplete penetrance,

variable expressivity, or partial dominance).

Symbolically, the pure idea of
dominance
(A3) plus independent, separately inherited alleles (A1,A2) does not always yield a uniform phenotype. Empirical data reveal that the same genotype can display different phenotypes under different conditions or developmental contexts.

Hence we reinterpret
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
collectively as:
there must be additional factors beyond simple genotype that modulate how strongly an allele’s trait is expressed
.

S_{0}=\{\,A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,B_{1}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\Bigl\{B_{1}:\text{Genotype alone does not fully determine phenotype;}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\text{variable expression implies regulatory influences.}\Bigr\}}

Rewrite Step 2: Introduce the Concept of Regulatory Control.

B_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{2},

where

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{any gene }g\text{ must be subject to some internal or external}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\text{control elements that adjust its level of expression}).

Symbolically, the fact that the same genetic allele (dominant or recessive) can manifest differently across tissues or conditions implies
regulatory control
—something “switches” or “modulates” gene output to produce variable phenotypes.

S_{1}=\{\,B_{1}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,B_{2}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,B_{2}\}}

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{a gene\textquoteright s activity depends on specific regulatory factors});\\

Rewrite Step 3: Specify the Molecular Basis of Regulation (Promoters, TFs, Enhancers, Epigenetics).

Finally, we identify
how
such regulatory modulation is realized at the molecular level, introducing elements like transcription factors (TFs), promoter and enhancer sequences, and epigenetic markers. Formally:

B_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;W_{4},

where

W_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,(\text{gene }g):\ \text{expression}(g)=f(\mathrm{TFs},\,\mathrm{promoters},\,\mathrm{enhancers},\,\mathrm{epigenetics}).

This is precisely the
Regulation of Gene Expression
axiom in molecular/genomic biology. It declares that a gene’s expression level (and thus its phenotypic impact) depends on various regulatory mechanisms—fully consistent with the classical observation that dominance alone cannot account for all variations in phenotype.

S_{2}=\{\,B_{2}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\,W_{4}\}.

Final Set (Molecular/Genomic Axiom 4: Regulation of Gene Expression):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{W_{4}:\ \forall\,(\text{gene }g):\ \text{expression}(g)=f(\mathrm{TFs},\mathrm{promoters},\mathrm{enhancers},\mathrm{epigenetics})\Bigr\}.}

Thus, from the classical principles of segregation, independent assortment, and dominance, we see that genotype-by-itself often fails to predict a uniform phenotype. In molecular terms, this necessitates
regulatory
factors that modulate gene expression, leading to the final statement that each gene’s expression is governed by interactions with transcription factors, promoter/enhancer regions, and epigenetic modifications.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,A_{1},\,A_{2},\,A_{3}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{1}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{2}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,W_{4}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Each rewrite step shows how classical genetic findings of variable phenotypic expression (despite known alleles and their dominance) logically necessitate
a molecular regulatory framework
, culminating in the
Regulation of Gene Expression
axiom.

Appendix N

From Euclidean Geometry to Hyperbolic Geometry

N.1

Euclidean Geometry (Hilbert Axioms Overview)

Euclidean geometry may be rigorously founded upon a set of 20 axioms famously formulated by David Hilbert. These axioms split into five major groups:

•

Incidence Axioms.
Govern how points, lines, and planes relate, ensuring basic existence properties (e.g., each line has points, each plane has noncollinear points, etc.).

•

Order (Betweenness) Axioms.
Introduce betweenness for points on a line and allow the definition of segments, rays, and the interior versus exterior of figures.

•

Congruence Axioms.
Provide rules for comparing lengths and angles, permitting one to construct equal segments and angles, and to prove when two triangles are congruent.

•

Continuity Axioms.
Impose an “Archimedean” condition ensuring no gaps exist in lengths or angles, enabling a robust notion of measurement.

•

Parallel Axiom (Playfair’s Axiom).
Given a line
\ell
and a point
P
not on
\ell
, there is exactly one line through
P
that does not meet
\ell
. Symbolically:

\forall\,\ell,\;\forall\,P\;\;\bigl(P\notin\ell\bigr)\;\Longrightarrow\;\exists!\,m\;\text{ such that }\;P\in m\;\text{ and }\;(m\cap\ell=\emptyset).

N.1.1

Completeness

These 20 axioms collectively furnish all the core properties of classical Euclidean geometry. From them, one can derive the well-known theorems on triangles, circles, polygons, and more. Hence, the system is
complete
in the sense that no essential geometric fact lies outside the scope of these axioms.

N.1.2

Independence

Each group introduces a unique concept that cannot be derived from the others:

•

Incidence (points, lines, planes) cannot emerge merely from order, congruence, continuity, or parallel statements.

•

Order (betweenness) contributes a distinct idea of “in-between” positions.

•

Congruence establishes measurement concepts of length and angle, irreducible to incidence and order alone.

•

Continuity ensures there are no gaps, something not guaranteed by incidence, order, or congruence.

•

The Parallel Axiom (Playfair’s) introduces the specifically Euclidean property that exactly one parallel passes through a given point not on a line.

No axiom can be proved solely from the others, so they form an
independent
foundation for Euclidean geometry.

N.2

Hyperbolic Geometry Axioms

Hyperbolic geometry
preserves the same Incidence, Order, Congruence, and Continuity Axioms as Euclidean geometry, but
replaces
the Euclidean Parallel Axiom (Playfair’s Axiom) with the
Hyperbolic Parallel Postulate
. In hyperbolic geometry, there are multiple lines through a point not on a given line that do not meet that given line. Symbolically, one version of this postulate can be stated:

\forall\,\ell,\;\forall\,P\hskip 9.24994pt\bigl(P\notin\ell\bigr)\;\Longrightarrow\;\exists\,m_{1}\neq m_{2}\;:P\in m_{1},\;P\in m_{2},\;m_{1}\cap\ell=\emptyset,\;m_{2}\cap\ell=\emptyset.

That is, there exist
at least two
distinct lines through
P
that do not intersect
\ell
.

N.2.1

Completeness

Since Hyperbolic geometry retains the same foundational statements regarding incidence, order, congruence, and continuity, it remains
complete
in that those axioms, combined with the Hyperbolic Parallel Postulate, suffice to describe a rich geometry of lines, angles, and figures without leaving any gaps. All standard hyperbolic theorems (e.g., angle sums of triangles being less than
180^{\circ}
) follow from these axioms.

N.2.2

Independence

Just as in the Euclidean case, each group of axioms remains independent:

•

The Hyperbolic Parallel Postulate cannot be derived from incidence, order, congruence, or continuity alone.

•

Changing precisely this one axiom from the Euclidean version yields a non-Euclidean geometry, demonstrating its logical independence.

Thus, hyperbolic geometry stands as a valid alternative to Euclidean geometry once the parallel axiom is altered.

N.3

Transformation

The only difference between hyperbolic and Euclidean geometry lies in the hyperbolic postulate.

N.3.1

Hyperbolic Postulate

Goal:
Transform the single
Playfair Axiom

\bigl(S_{0}\bigr):\;\forall\,\ell,\;\forall\,P\;\bigl(P\notin\ell\bigr)\;\Longrightarrow\;\exists!\,m\;:\;\bigl(P\in m\bigr)\;\wedge\;\bigl(m\cap\ell=\emptyset\bigr)

into a
Hyperbolic Parallel Postulate

\bigl(S_{2}\bigr):\;\forall\,\ell,\;\forall\,P\;\bigl(P\notin\ell\bigr)\;\Longrightarrow\;\exists\,m_{1},\,m_{2}:\;m_{1}\neq m_{2},\;P\in m_{1},\;P\in m_{2},\;m_{1}\cap\ell=\emptyset,\;m_{2}\cap\ell=\emptyset.

In words, we replace “
\exists!\,m
” (exactly one parallel line through
P
) with “there exist at least two distinct parallels through
P
.”

Rewrite Step 1: Remove the ‘Exactly One’ Requirement.

Starting from

S_{0}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,\ell,\;\forall\,P\;\bigl(P\notin\ell\bigr)\;\Longrightarrow\;\exists!\,m:\;P\in m\;\wedge\;m\cap\ell=\emptyset,

we
weaken
the statement to say only
some
parallel line exists. Symbolically,

S_{0}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,\ell,\;\forall\,P\;\bigl(P\notin\ell\bigr)\;\Longrightarrow\;\exists\,m:\;P\in m\;\wedge\;m\cap\ell=\emptyset.

Here, “
\exists!\,m
” becomes “
\exists\,m
,” eliminating uniqueness but retaining the idea that
at least one
parallel line exists.

Rewrite Step 2: Impose Multiple Parallels.

To obtain a
hyperbolic
statement, we
strengthen

S_{1}
to require
at least two
parallels:

S_{1}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}:

S_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,\ell,\;\forall\,P\;\bigl(P\notin\ell\bigr)\;\Longrightarrow\;\exists\,m_{1}\neq m_{2},\hskip 9.24994ptP\in m_{1},\hskip 9.24994ptP\in m_{2},\hskip 9.24994ptm_{1}\cap\ell=\emptyset,\hskip 9.24994ptm_{2}\cap\ell=\emptyset.

Symbolically,

S_{2}:\,\text{\textquotedblleft At least two distinct lines through $P$ do not meet $\ell$.''}

This is precisely the
Hyperbolic Parallel Postulate
.

Final Set (Hyperbolic Version of Playfair’s Axiom):

\boxed{S_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,\ell,\;\forall\,P\;\bigl(P\notin\ell\bigr)\;\Longrightarrow\;\exists\,m_{1}\neq m_{2}\;:\;P\in m_{1},\;P\in m_{2},\;m_{1}\cap\ell=\emptyset,\;m_{2}\cap\ell=\emptyset.}

Symbolic Evolution (Compact View):

\underbrace{(\exists!\,\text{m: unique parallel})}_{S_{0}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{(\exists\,\text{m: at least one parallel})}_{S_{1}}\;\longrightarrow\;\underbrace{(\exists\,m_{1},m_{2}:\text{ at least two parallels})}_{S_{2}}.

N.3.2

Comment on Other Modifications

If, instead of altering the parallel axiom
\mathcal{P}
, we
modify other Hilbert axioms
, we obtain different geometrical systems:

•

Elliptical (Riemannian) Geometry
: One typical approach is to replace the parallel axiom with “no parallels exist,” or to alter the incidence structure so that any two lines meet in exactly two points (projective-like).

•

Projective Geometries
: Further changes to incidence and order axioms can collapse distance notions but keep cross-ratio invariants.

In each case, Hilbert’s axioms can be only partially changed, but the geometry’s overall nature (Euclidean, hyperbolic, elliptical, projective, etc.) critically depends on how we
replace or alter
one or more of the standard statements.

Appendix O

From Classical Fourier Analysis to Wavelet Theory

O.1

Classical Fourier Analysis

Classical Fourier Analysis studies how functions can be represented (or approximated) by sums (or integrals) of simpler basis functions, typically exponentials or sines and cosines.
Below is a minimal and complete set of axioms, each of which is independent (i.e. cannot be derived from the others) and collectively sufficient to derive the main results of classical Fourier expansions: orthogonality, uniqueness of coefficients, completeness in suitable function spaces, etc.

O.1.1

Axioms

1.

Periodicity & Integrability

\forall\,f:\hskip 9.24994pt\Bigl(f\text{ is }2\pi\text{-periodic}\;\wedge\;f\in L^{1}[-\pi,\pi]\Bigr).

We assume each considered function
f
is
2\pi
-periodic and integrable (e.g. piecewise continuous or in
L^{1}[-\pi,\pi]
). This ensures integrals of
f
over one period are well-defined.

2.

Orthogonality of Exponential Basis

\forall\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z},\hskip 9.24994pt\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{\,i\,m\,x}\;e^{-\,i\,n\,x}\,dx\;=\;2\pi\,\delta_{m,n},

where
\delta_{m,n}
is the Kronecker delta (
=1
if
m=n
,
0
otherwise).
This states that the set
\{e^{\,i\,n\,x}\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
forms an orthogonal system on
[-\pi,\pi]
.

3.

Fourier Coefficient Formula

\forall\,f,\hskip 9.24994ptc_{n}\;=\;\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(x)\;e^{-\,i\,n\,x}\;dx\hskip 9.24994pt(\forall\,n\in\mathbb{Z}).

Each
f
admits coefficients
(c_{n})
given by the above integral.
In some formulations,
n
ranges over integers for exponential expansions, or includes separate sine/cosine expansions.

4.

Fourier Series Representation

f(x)\;\sim\;\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}c_{n}\;e^{\,i\,n\,x},

meaning
f(x)
is represented (pointwise, or in some norm sense) by the infinite series whose
n
th term is
c_{n}\,e^{\,i\,n\,x}
.
Under suitable conditions (e.g. Dirichlet, Jordan, or
L^{2}
criteria), this series converges to
f
in the chosen sense.

O.1.2

Completeness

1.

Periodicity & Integrability.
(Axiom 1) sets the domain and guarantees well-defined integrals over one period.

2.

Orthogonality & Coefficients.
Axiom 2 and Axiom 3 provide a systematic way to compute coefficients from an orthogonal exponential basis, ensuring each
f
has a unique set
\{c_{n}\}
.

3.

Series Representation.
(Axiom 4) states that
f
can be expressed as a (possibly infinite) sum of the exponential basis functions weighted by these coefficients, completing the idea of a Fourier expansion.

From these four, one can derive the fundamental properties of Fourier series: uniqueness of coefficients, Parseval’s identity, pointwise/uniform/
L^{2}
convergence (under specific theorems), and expansions of periodic signals in engineering and physics contexts.

O.1.3

Independence

1.

Periodicity & Integrability.
cannot be deduced from orthogonality, coefficient formulas, or the final series statement; it specifically restricts the function’s domain and integrable property, laying the groundwork for meaningful integrals.

2.

Orthogonality of Exponential Basis.
cannot be inferred from the other axioms. It must be stated that
\{e^{\,i\,n\,x}\}
is orthogonal under the integral inner product, as this is not implied by the mere existence of a periodic integrable function or a coefficient formula.

3.

Fourier Coefficient Formula.
is not derivable from periodicity, orthogonality alone, or the final series representation. One must explicitly define how
c_{n}
are computed from
f
.

4.

Fourier Series Representation.
does not follow from any combination of periodic integrability, orthogonality, or the definition of coefficients. It is an additional statement that such a series
does
represent (or approximate)
f
in a valid sense, and thus must be stated as a separate principle.

O.2

Wavelet Theory

Wavelet Theory provides a framework for analyzing functions (or signals) at multiple scales via a family of translates and dilates of a single “mother wavelet.” Below is a minimal and complete set of axioms, each of which is independent (i.e. it cannot be derived from the others) and collectively sufficient to derive standard wavelet expansions, orthogonality relations, and multi-resolution decompositions.

O.2.1

Axioms

1.

Mother Wavelet & Integrability

\exists\,\psi:\hskip 9.24994pt\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\psi(x)\,dx=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\psi(x)|\,dx<\infty.

There is a single function (the “mother wavelet”)
\psi(x)
with zero mean and finite integral norm, ensuring local oscillation and admissibility.

2.

Dilation & Translation

\forall\,j\in\mathbb{Z},\;\forall\,k\in\mathbb{Z},\hskip 9.24994pt\psi_{j,k}(x)\;=\;2^{\tfrac{j}{2}}\,\psi\!\bigl(2^{j}\,x-k\bigr).

A scaled and shifted family is constructed from
\psi
, parameterized by integer
j
(scale) and
k
(position). This defines the wavelet system
\{\psi_{j,k}\}
.

3.

Orthogonality (or Tight Frame)

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\psi_{j,k}(x)\,\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}(x)\;dx\;=\;\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\,\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}.

For an
orthonormal wavelet
, distinct wavelets in
\{\psi_{j,k}\}
are pairwise orthogonal in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. (In a more general frame setting, one replaces this with a tight-frame condition.)

4.

Wavelet Expansion

\forall\,f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}):\hskip 9.24994ptf(x)\;=\;\sum_{j,k\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigl\langle f,\,\psi_{j,k}\bigr\rangle\,\psi_{j,k}(x),

where the series converges in
L^{2}
norm (or pointwise under additional regularity). Every square-integrable function
f
can be reconstructed from its wavelet coefficients
\langle f,\psi_{j,k}\rangle
via the orthonormal (or tight) wavelet basis.

O.2.2

Completeness

1.

Mother Wavelet & Integrability.
(Axiom 1) imposes the zero-mean and finite-energy properties necessary for localization in both time (or space) and frequency domains.

2.

Dilation & Translation.
(Axiom 2) organizes the wavelet system by scaling and shifting
\psi
, creating a multi-scale family of analyzing functions.

3.

Orthogonality.
(Axiom 3) ensures each
\psi_{j,k}
is orthonormal to all others, a key property for simplification of expansions and coefficient computations.

4.

Wavelet Expansion.
(Axiom 4) completes the theory by stating that any function (in
L^{2}
) can be expressed as a linear combination of these wavelets, ensuring a full basis for signal reconstruction.

From these four, one obtains the standard results of wavelet theory: multi-resolution analysis, fast wavelet transforms, and efficient representation of signals across multiple scales.

O.2.3

Independence

1.

Mother Wavelet & Integrability.
cannot be deduced from dilation/translation structure, orthogonality, or the final expansion statement. It sets the fundamental shape and integrable nature of the wavelet, a separate requirement.

2.

Dilation & Translation.
is not derivable from zero-mean integrability, orthogonality conditions, or the statement of expansions. It must be explicitly stipulated that
\psi
generates a family via scale and shift.

3.

Orthogonality.
does not follow from mother wavelet assumptions, nor from simply dilating/translating
\psi
. It is a special structural property (or in general, a tight-frame condition) that must be imposed.

4.

Wavelet Expansion.
cannot be concluded from integrability, the existence of a dilated/translated family, or orthogonality alone. It is an additional global claim that every
L^{2}
function can be represented in this wavelet basis.

O.3

Transformations

O.3.1

Axiom 1

Goal:
Transform a subset of Classical Fourier axioms

\bigl\{\,F_{1}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994ptF_{1}:\,\text{(Periodicity \& Integrability)},

into the
Wavelet Theory
statement:

W_{1}:\exists\,\psi:\hskip 9.24994pt\bigl(\int\psi(x)\,dx=0\bigr)\;\wedge\;\Bigl(\int|\psi(x)|\,dx<\infty\Bigr),

which is the
Mother Wavelet & Integrability
axiom. Symbolically, we show how requiring a function to be integrable (and suitably “mean-free”) transitions to a wavelet satisfying zero mean and finite energy/norm requirements.

Initial Axiom (Fourier, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{F_{1}\bigr\}}

where

F_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{each }f\text{ is }2\pi\text{-periodic})\;\wedge\;\bigl(f\in L^{1}[-\pi,\pi]\bigr).

“Each function
f
is integrable over one period (e.g.
L^{1}[-\pi,\pi]
) and repeats with period
2\pi
.”

Rewrite Step 1: Flatten Periodicity to Single Period’s “Zero-Average” Variant.

F_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;F_{1}^{\prime},

where

F_{1}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,f,\;\Bigl(\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(x)\,dx<\infty\Bigr)\;\Longrightarrow\;\Bigl(\text{one can choose }f\text{ with}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(x)\,dx=0\Bigr).

Symbolically, we rewrite the
2\pi
-periodic integrable function so that its integral over one fundamental domain is zero. (One can always subtract its average to force
\int f=0
without losing integrability.)

S_{0}=\{\,F_{1}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,F_{1}^{\prime}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\,F_{1}^{\prime}\}}

F_{1}^{\prime}:\;\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(x)\,dx=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\text{with }f\in L^{1}[-\pi,\pi].

Now we have a function of zero average (over a single period) and still integrable.

Rewrite Step 2: Extend Domain from
[-\pi,\pi]
to
\mathbb{R}
.

F_{1}^{\prime}\;\longrightarrow\;G_{1},

where

G_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\psi(x)\;=\;\begin{cases}f(x),&x\in[-\pi,\pi],\\
0,&x\notin[-\pi,\pi],\end{cases}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{with }\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(x)\,dx=0,\;\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\psi(x)|\,dx<\infty.

Symbolically, we
unfold

f
from one period into a function
\psi
on the entire real line by defining
\psi(x)=f(x)
on
[-\pi,\pi]
and
\psi(x)=0
elsewhere.
Thus
\psi
has zero integral over
\mathbb{R}
and is still integrable in the absolute sense.

S_{1}=\{\,F_{1}^{\prime}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,G_{1}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,G_{1}\}}

G_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\psi(x)\;=\;\begin{cases}f(x),&x\in[-\pi,\pi]\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},\hskip 9.24994pt\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(x)\,dx=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\psi(x)|\,dx<\infty.

So
\psi
is a candidate for a function with zero mean and finite
L^{1}
norm on
\mathbb{R}
.

Rewrite Step 3: Identify
\psi
as “Mother Wavelet” with Zero Mean & Integrability.

Finally, we interpret
\psi
from
G_{1}
as the
mother wavelet
:

G_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;W_{1},

where

W_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\exists\,\psi:\ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\psi(x)\,dx=0\hskip 9.24994pt\wedge\hskip 9.24994pt\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\psi(x)|\,dx<\infty.

This precisely matches the
Mother Wavelet & Integrability
axiom: the wavelet must have a zero integral (“admissibility” or no DC component) and be in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
(or
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
in stronger settings).

S_{2}=\{\,G_{1}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\,W_{1}\}.

Final Set (Wavelet Axiom 1: Mother Wavelet & Integrability):

\boxed{S_{3}=\bigl\{W_{1}:\;\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}),\;\int\psi(x)\,dx=0\bigr\}.}

Hence, by modifying the periodic integrable function
f
(Fourier domain) into a compactly supported or absolutely integrable function
\psi
(wavelet domain) with zero mean, we obtain the mother wavelet conditions.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,F_{1}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,F_{1}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,G_{1}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,W_{1}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Thus, starting from the
Periodic & Integrable
condition in Fourier analysis,
we successively rewrite
f
into a function
\psi
on
\mathbb{R}
with
zero mean
and
finite integral norm
, fulfilling the mother wavelet requirements of Wavelet Axiom 1.

O.3.2

Axiom 2

Goal:
Transform a subset of Classical Fourier axioms

\bigl\{F_{2}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994ptF_{2}:\ \text{Orthogonality of Exponential Basis: }\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{i\,m\,x}e^{-\,i\,n\,x}\,dx=2\pi\,\delta_{m,n},

into the
Wavelet Theory
statement:

W_{2}:\ \forall\,(j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},\hskip 9.24994pt\psi_{j,k}(x)=2^{\tfrac{j}{2}}\,\psi\bigl(2^{j}\,x-k\bigr).

This is the
Dilation & Translation
axiom, where a single mother wavelet
\psi
generates a discrete family
\{\psi_{j,k}\}
by scaling and shifting.

Initial Axiom (Fourier, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{F_{2}\bigr\}}

where

F_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}:\hskip 9.24994pt\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{\,i\,m\,x}\;e^{-\,i\,n\,x}\,dx=2\pi\,\delta_{m,n}.

This states that
\bigl\{e^{\,i\,n\,x}\bigr\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
forms an orthogonal (indeed orthonormal up to the factor
\sqrt{2\pi}
) set on
[-\pi,\pi]
.

Rewrite Step 1: Re-Indexing from Integer
n
to a Multi-Index
(j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

F_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;F_{2}^{\prime},

where

F_{2}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\bigl\{e^{\,i\,n\,x}\bigr\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mapsto\hskip 9.24994pt\bigl\{\phi_{j,k}(x)\bigr\}_{(j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}.

Symbolically, we expand the single integer
n
into two integers
j,k
. We may think of
j
as capturing a “scale” index and
k
as a “shift” index, but we have not yet specified how
\phi_{j,k}
depends on
x
.

S_{0}=\{\,F_{2}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,F_{2}^{\prime}\}.

F_{2}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\phi_{j,k}\leftrightarrow(\text{some function indexed by }j,k),\hskip 9.24994pt\text{preserving orthogonality}

\text{under an integral inner product}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\,F_{2}^{\prime}\}}

F_{2}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\phi_{j,k},\,\phi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\rangle=0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{if}\hskip 9.24994pt(j,k)\neq(j^{\prime},k^{\prime}),

with
\langle f,g\rangle=\int f(x)\,\overline{g(x)}\,dx
.
(Exact normalization is deferred.)

Rewrite Step 2: Replace Complex Exponentials by a Localized Prototype.

F_{2}^{\prime}\;\longrightarrow\;G_{2},

where

G_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\phi_{j,k}(x)=\phi(2^{j}\,x-k),\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for some single prototype function }\phi.

Here we interpret the integer
j
as a dilation factor (multiplies
x
by
2^{j}
) and
k
as a translation.
We have replaced the global exponential
e^{\,i\,n\,x}
(extending over all
[-\pi,\pi]
or
\mathbb{R}
) with a local
wave-shape

\phi
that is stretched and shifted.

S_{1}=\{\,F_{2}^{\prime}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,G_{2}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,G_{2}\}}

G_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\phi_{j,k}(x)=\phi\bigl(2^{j}\,x-k\bigr),\hskip 9.24994pt\text{with some function }\phi.

We still lack a normalization factor, but have the notion of discrete scale
j
and shift
k
.

Rewrite Step 3: Introduce the
2^{j/2}
Normalization Factor.

G_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;W_{2},

where

W_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\psi_{j,k}(x)=2^{\tfrac{j}{2}}\,\psi\!\bigl(2^{j}\,x-k\bigr).

This enforces orthonormality (or tight framing) in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
The factor
2^{j/2}
precisely balances the scale effect so that each dilated version has the same
L^{2}
norm.
Here we identify
\psi\equiv\phi
, now called the
mother wavelet
, in alignment with wavelet theory.

S_{2}=\{\,G_{2}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\,W_{2}\}.

Final Set (Wavelet Axiom 2: Dilation & Translation):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{W_{2}:\ \forall\,(j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:\ \psi_{j,k}(x)=2^{\tfrac{j}{2}}\psi\bigl(2^{j}\,x-k\bigr)\Bigr\}.}

Thus, the single integer index
n
from Fourier exponentials is replaced by two indices
(j,k)
for wavelets, representing discrete scale and shift. The amplitude factor
2^{j/2}
ensures normalized energy across different scales.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,F_{2}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,F_{2}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,G_{2}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,W_{2}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Interpretation of Each Step:

1.

Re-index
from
n\in\mathbb{Z}
to
(j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

to allow a two-dimensional (scale, shift) parameter space.

2.

Replace exponentials
by a
localized
function
\phi

subject to dilation and translation.

3.

Introduce the
2^{j/2}
factor
to preserve
L^{2}
-norm consistency
across scales, yielding the standard wavelet family.

Hence, we arrive at the wavelet system

\{\psi_{j,k}\}
from the original concept of a discrete orthogonal set

\{e^{\,i\,n\,x}\}.

O.3.3

Axiom 3

Goal:
Transform a subset of Classical Fourier axioms

\bigl\{F_{3}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994ptF_{3}:\ \text{Fourier Coefficients }\bigl(c_{n}=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\!\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(x)\,e^{-\,i\,n\,x}\,dx\bigr),

into the
Wavelet Theory
statement:

W_{3}:\ \forall\,(j,k)\neq(j^{\prime},k^{\prime}):\;\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\psi_{j,k}(x)\,\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}(x)\,dx\;=\;0,

i.e. the
Orthogonality of the wavelet family

\{\psi_{j,k}\}
.
Symbolically, we show how the computation of unique Fourier coefficients
implies an
orthonormal
wavelet set, where each pair of distinct wavelets
has zero overlap (inner product).

Initial Axiom (Fourier, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{F_{3}\bigr\}}

where

F_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,f,\hskip 9.24994ptc_{n}\;=\;\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(x)\;e^{-\,i\,n\,x}\,dx,\hskip 9.24994ptn\in\mathbb{Z}.

This prescribes how to compute the unique coefficient
c_{n}
from
f
.
Orthogonality of exponentials (Axiom 2) ensures that these
c_{n}
are distinct
and well-defined for each integer
n
.

Rewrite Step 1: Re-interpret Coefficients As “Inner Products.”

F_{3}\;\longrightarrow\;F_{3}^{\prime},

where

F_{3}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994ptc_{n}=\langle f,\,e^{\,i\,n\,x}\rangle_{[-\pi,\pi]},

with

\langle g,h\rangle_{[-\pi,\pi]}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}g(x)\,\overline{h(x)}\,dx.

Symbolically,
c_{n}
is the inner product of
f
with the basis function

e^{\,i\,n\,x}
. This viewpoint paves the way for generalizing
to other orthonormal systems.

S_{0}=\{\,F_{3}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,F_{3}^{\prime}\}.

F_{3}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994ptc_{n}=\langle f,\,\phi_{n}\rangle,\hskip 9.24994pt\phi_{n}(x)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,e^{\,i\,n\,x},

(optional renormalization) so
\{\phi_{n}\}
is an orthonormal set
over
[-\pi,\pi]
.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\,F_{3}^{\prime}\}}

F_{3}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994ptc_{n}=\langle f,\phi_{n}\rangle,\hskip 9.24994pt\phi_{n}(x)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,e^{\,i\,n\,x}.

Hence, the coefficient formula is recast in inner-product terms.

Rewrite Step 2: Generalize “Coefficient Formula” to an Orthogonal System
\{\psi_{\alpha}\}
.

F_{3}^{\prime}\;\longrightarrow\;G_{3},

where

G_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,f,\hskip 9.24994ptc_{\alpha}=\langle f,\,\psi_{\alpha}\rangle,

with
\alpha
indexing an orthonormal basis
\{\psi_{\alpha}\}
.
Here we replace the integer index
n
with a general index
\alpha
(which might be multi-dimensional, e.g.
(j,k)
).
We still retain the key property that distinct basis elements are
orthonormal
:

\langle\psi_{\alpha},\;\psi_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rangle=\delta_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}.

Thus, every function
f
can be broken into coefficients
c_{\alpha}
via inner products with a basis
\psi_{\alpha}
.

S_{1}=\{\,F_{3}^{\prime}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,G_{3}\}.

G_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\psi_{\alpha}\in\text{ONB (orthonormal basis)},\hskip 9.24994ptc_{\alpha}=\langle f,\psi_{\alpha}\rangle.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,G_{3}\}}

G_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Given an orthonormal family }\{\psi_{\alpha}\},\;\text{the coefficient of }f\text{ is }c_{\alpha}=\langle f,\psi_{\alpha}\rangle.

We have not yet specified
which
orthonormal system or how
\alpha
is structured.

Rewrite Step 3: Specialize
\alpha=(j,k)
to Wavelets
\{\psi_{j,k}\}
, Enforce Orthogonality.

G_{3}\;\longrightarrow\;W_{3},

where

W_{3}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,(j,k)\neq(j^{\prime},k^{\prime}):\hskip 9.24994pt\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\psi_{j,k}(x)\,\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}(x)\,dx=0.

This is the wavelet
Orthogonality Axiom
: distinct wavelets

\psi_{j,k}
and
\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}
have zero inner product.
Equivalently,
\{\psi_{j,k}\}
forms an
orthonormal
system in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

S_{2}=\{\,G_{3}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\,W_{3}\}.

Final Set (Wavelet Axiom 3: Orthogonality):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{W_{3}:\ \langle\psi_{j,k},\,\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\rangle=\int\psi_{j,k}(x)\,\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}(x)\,dx=\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\,\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}\Bigr\}.}

Thus, from the idea of
unique coefficients
in Fourier expansions
(
c_{n}=\langle f,e^{\,i\,n\,x}\rangle
), we generalize to an arbitrary
orthonormal family, then specify it to wavelets
\psi_{j,k}
that remain
mutually orthogonal for different
(j,k)
.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,F_{3}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,F_{3}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,G_{3}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,W_{3}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Interpretation of Each Step:

1.

Rewrite the coefficient formula
in terms of an inner product with a basis function.

2.

Generalize
from exponentials to any orthonormal family
\{\psi_{\alpha}\}
.

3.

Specialize
to wavelets
\psi_{j,k}
, asserting orthogonality across scales and shifts, giving the wavelet orthogonality axiom.

Hence, the uniqueness of Fourier coefficients morphs into the wavelet requirement that each basis element
\psi_{j,k}
is orthogonal to all the others in the wavelet system.

O.3.4

Axiom 4

Goal:
Transform the classical
Fourier Series Representation

\bigl\{F_{4}\bigr\}\hskip 9.24994ptF_{4}:\ f(x)\;\sim\;\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}c_{n}\,e^{\,i\,n\,x},

into the
Wavelet Theory
statement:

W_{4}:\ f(x)=\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\langle f,\psi_{j,k}\rangle\,\psi_{j,k}(x).

That is, every function
f
(in a suitable function space, typically
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
) can be written as a superposition of wavelets
\{\psi_{j,k}\}
.

Initial Axiom (Fourier, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{F_{4}\bigr\}}

where

F_{4}:\hskip 9.24994ptf(x)\;\sim\;\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}c_{n}\,e^{\,i\,n\,x},

and
c_{n}
are given by the coefficient formula
c_{n}=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(x)\,e^{-\,i\,n\,x}\,dx
.
This states that
f
can be reconstructed (pointwise, in
L^{2}
, or in other senses) from its infinite sum of harmonics.

Rewrite Step 1: Interpret Sum Over
n
as an Orthonormal Expansion.

F_{4}\;\longrightarrow\;F_{4}^{\prime},

where

F_{4}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994ptf(x)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\langle f,\phi_{n}\rangle\,\phi_{n}(x),\hskip 9.24994pt\phi_{n}(x)\;=\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,e^{\,i\,n\,x},

so that
\{\phi_{n}\}
is an orthonormal system on
[-\pi,\pi]
.
Symbolically, we rewrite the Fourier series as a general orthonormal expansion:

f=\sum_{n}\langle f,\phi_{n}\rangle\,\phi_{n}.

This step clarifies that each partial sum is the projection onto the subspace spanned by
\{\phi_{n}\}
.

S_{0}=\{\,F_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}=\{\,F_{4}^{\prime}\}.

F_{4}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{For }f\in L^{2}[-\pi,\pi],\;f=\sum_{n}\langle f,\phi_{n}\rangle\phi_{n},\;\phi_{n}(x)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,e^{\,i\,n\,x}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\,F_{4}^{\prime}\}}

F_{4}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994ptf=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\langle f,\phi_{n}\rangle\,\phi_{n},\hskip 9.24994pt(\phi_{n}\text{ orthonormal basis on }[-\pi,\pi]).

Rewrite Step 2: Extend Domain from
[-\pi,\pi]
to
\mathbb{R}
and Replace
\{\phi_{n}\}
by a More General ONB
\{\psi_{\alpha}\}
.

F_{4}^{\prime}\;\longrightarrow\;G_{4},

where

G_{4}:\hskip 9.24994ptf(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\langle f,\psi_{\alpha}\rangle\,\psi_{\alpha}(x),

with
\{\psi_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
an orthonormal basis in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. We switch from the integer index
n
on
[-\pi,\pi]
to a potentially more general index set
A
on the entire real line
\mathbb{R}
.

S_{1}=\{\,F_{4}^{\prime}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{2}=\{\,G_{4}\}.

G_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Any }f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\text{ has a unique expansion in some ONB }\{\psi_{\alpha}\}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,G_{4}\}}

G_{4}:\hskip 9.24994ptf=\sum_{\alpha\in A}\langle f,\psi_{\alpha}\rangle\,\psi_{\alpha}(x),\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\psi_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rangle=\delta_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}.

Still, we have not specified the structure of
A
nor the special wavelet form of
\psi_{\alpha}
.

Rewrite Step 3: Specialize to the Wavelet Family
\{\psi_{j,k}\}
and State Complete Series.

G_{4}\;\longrightarrow\;W_{4},

where

W_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\hskip 9.24994ptf(x)=\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\langle f,\psi_{j,k}\rangle\;\psi_{j,k}(x),

and
\{\psi_{j,k}\}
is the discrete wavelet basis defined by

\psi_{j,k}(x)=2^{j/2}\,\psi\bigl(2^{j}\,x-k\bigr),

with
\langle\psi_{j,k},\,\psi_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\rangle=0
whenever
(j,k)\neq(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})
.
This is precisely the
Wavelet Expansion
axiom, stating that every
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
function can be reconstructed from its wavelet coefficients.

S_{2}=\{\,G_{4}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\longrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptS_{3}=\{\,W_{4}\}.

Final Set (Wavelet Axiom 4: Wavelet Expansion):

\boxed{S_{3}=\Bigl\{W_{4}:\hskip 9.24994ptf(x)=\sum_{j,k\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle f,\psi_{j,k}\rangle\;\psi_{j,k}(x)\Bigr\}.}

Hence, the classical notion of representing
f
as a sum of exponentials over
[-\pi,\pi]
generalizes to a representation via
localized
wavelets
\psi_{j,k}
across
\mathbb{R}
.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,F_{4}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,F_{4}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,G_{4}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,W_{4}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Interpretation of Each Step:

1.

Rewrite Fourier sum
as an orthonormal expansion
\sum_{n}\langle f,\phi_{n}\rangle\,\phi_{n}
.

2.

Extend
to a general orthonormal basis
\{\psi_{\alpha}\}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, removing the
2\pi
-periodic domain restriction.

3.

Restrict to wavelet basis

\{\psi_{j,k}\}
, yielding a discrete sum over scales and shifts that covers
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Thus, the final statement
W_{4}
proclaims a full wavelet expansion for any square-integrable function, analogous to a Fourier series but in time-frequency localized wavelets.

Appendix P

From Classical Chemistry to Bohr Atomic Theory

P.1

Classical Chemistry

Classical Chemistry studies how elements combine and transform into compounds via chemical reactions, under a set of fundamental empirical laws. Below is a minimal and complete set of axioms, each of which is independent (i.e., it cannot be derived from the others) and collectively sufficient to describe basic chemical reactions, composition of compounds, and the behavior of gases under standard conditions.

P.1.1

Axioms

1.

Atomic Theory

\exists\text{ fundamental units }(\text{atoms})\ \forall\ \text{elements},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{each element has unique atomic mass }m_{A}.

Matter consists of fundamental, indivisible units called atoms, each associated with a specific element and a unique atomic mass.

2.

Conservation of Mass

\forall\,\text{Reaction }R:\hskip 9.24994pt\sum_{i\in\mathrm{Reactants}}m_{i}\;=\;\sum_{j\in\mathrm{Products}}m_{j},

where
m_{i}
is the total mass of reactant
i
and
m_{j}
is the total mass of product
j
.
No net mass is lost or gained in an isolated chemical reaction.

3.

Definite Composition

\forall\,\text{Compound }C:\hskip 9.24994pt\exists\,(\mathrm{elements}\ E_{1},\dots,E_{k})\hskip 9.24994pt\text{such that}\hskip 9.24994pt\dfrac{m(E_{1})}{m(E_{2})}=\text{constant},

for a fixed ratio of masses of any two elements
E_{1},E_{2}
in
C
.
A pure chemical compound always contains the same elements in the same proportions by mass.

4.

Multiple Proportions

\forall\,(\mathrm{elements}\ A,B),\ \forall\,(\text{compounds }C_{1},C_{2}\text{ of }A,B):\hskip 9.24994pt\dfrac{m(B)_{C_{1}}}{m(A)_{C_{1}}}\;\Big/\;\dfrac{m(B)_{C_{2}}}{m(A)_{C_{2}}}=\dfrac{p}{q},

where
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
are small integers.
If two elements form more than one compound, then the ratio of the masses of one element that combine with a fixed mass of the other are in ratios of small whole numbers.

5.

Avogadro’s Law (Classical Form)

\forall\,(\mathrm{gases}\ G_{1},G_{2})\ \forall\,V>0,\ (\mathrm{same}\ T,P):\hskip 9.24994pt\dfrac{N(G_{1},V)}{N(G_{2},V)}=1,

where
N(G_{i},V)
is the number of “molecular particles” of gas
G_{i}
occupying volume
V
at the same temperature
T
and pressure
P
.
Equal volumes of any ideal gases, under the same conditions of temperature and pressure, contain the same number of particles.

P.1.2

Completeness

1.

Atomic Theory.
( Axiom 1) establishes that matter is composed of fundamental, indivisible atoms, which serve as the basis for all chemical reactions and mass relationships.

2.

Conservation of Mass.
( Axiom 2) ensures that in a closed system, mass is neither created nor destroyed during chemical reactions.

3.

Definite Composition & Multiple Proportions.
Axiom 3 and Axiom 4 describe how elements combine in fixed, well-defined ratios by mass, and how multiple distinct compounds from the same elements exhibit integer mass-ratio relationships.

4.

Avogadro’s Law.
( Axiom 5) extends these foundational mass relationships to the realm of gases, providing a direct link between macroscopic volumes and microscopic particle counts under uniform conditions.

These five axioms together allow prediction of reaction stoichiometry, compound formulas, and gas behavior in classical chemistry. They suffice to explain much of 19th-century chemical observations, paving the way for more advanced atomic and molecular theories.

P.1.3

Independence

1.

Atomic Theory.
cannot be deduced from mass conservation, definite composition, multiple proportions, or Avogadro’s law. It asserts the discrete nature of matter, which is an independent foundational concept.

2.

Conservation of Mass.
cannot be deduced from atomic theory, definite composition, multiple proportions, or Avogadro’s law, which all presume mass-based or volumetric combinations of elements but do not guarantee total mass constancy in reactions.

3.

Definite Composition.
is not derivable from mass conservation, atomic theory, multiple proportions, or Avogadro’s law alone. It specifically states each compound has a fixed mass ratio of elements, a separate assertion from the invariance or ratio rules of other axioms.

4.

Multiple Proportions.
cannot be obtained from atomic theory, mass conservation, or definite composition alone (each compound’s internal ratio is fixed, but this does not imply the
integer ratio
pattern across multiple compounds) nor from Avogadro’s law (gas volume relationships do not address integer mass ratios in distinct compounds).

5.

Avogadro’s Law.
does not follow from any combination of the other four axioms. Conservation of mass and elemental ratio axioms describe solid and solution-phase compound formation but do not dictate how gas volumes relate to particle numbers.

P.2

Bohr Atomic Theory

Bohr’s atomic theory introduces quantized electron orbits around a central nucleus, combining classical physics with early quantum postulates. It explains why atoms emit or absorb energy in discrete amounts (spectral lines) and sets the stage for modern quantum chemistry.

P.2.1

Axioms

1.

Stable Orbits

\forall\,(\text{electron }e\text{ in atom }A):\hskip 9.24994pte\text{ moves in circular orbits about }A\text{\textquoteright s nucleus}

\text{without radiating electromagnetic energy, whenever in an allowed orbit}.

Electrons orbit the nucleus under electrostatic attraction, and do not continuously emit energy (as classical electrodynamics would predict) if they occupy these specific, stable orbits.

2.

Angular Momentum Quantization

L_{n}=n\;\frac{h}{2\pi},\hskip 9.24994ptn\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

where
L_{n}
is the orbital angular momentum of an electron in the
n
th allowed orbit, and
h
is Planck’s constant. Only integral multiples of
h/(2\pi)
are permitted.

3.

Energy Level Postulate

E_{n}=-\,\frac{Z^{2}\,e^{2}}{8\pi\epsilon_{0}\,a_{0}}\,\frac{1}{n^{2}},\hskip 9.24994ptn\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

for a hydrogen-like atom of nuclear charge
Ze
. Each allowed orbit corresponds to a specific quantized energy
E_{n}
. (In the simplest hydrogen case,
Z=1
and
a_{0}
is the Bohr radius.)

4.

Spectral Transition Condition

\Delta E=E_{m}-E_{n}=h\,\nu,

where
\nu
is the frequency of the emitted (or absorbed) photon. An electron transitioning between orbit
m
and
n
releases or absorbs a photon of energy
h\,\nu
equal to the difference in orbital energies.

P.2.2

Completeness

1.

Stable Orbits.
(Axiom 1) exempts electrons from classical radiation loss in these special orbits, preventing them from spiraling into the nucleus.

2.

Quantization (Angular Momentum & Energy Levels).
Axiom 2 and Axiom 3 specify how electron orbits are discretized in angular momentum and corresponding energies, addressing why only certain atomic radii/energies are observed.

3.

Spectral Transitions.
(Axiom 4) connects these discrete energy levels to observed emission/absorption lines. It explains how atomic spectra arise from electrons jumping between quantized states, each jump releasing or absorbing a photon of specific energy.

Together, these four axioms suffice to predict the Rydberg formula for the hydrogen spectrum, explain atomic stability, and match key experimental data on line spectra. They form the foundation of Bohr’s model, an essential stepping-stone to full quantum mechanics.

P.2.3

Independence

1.

Stable Orbits.
cannot be deduced from angular-momentum quantization, energy-level discretization, or spectral transitions alone. It specifically states that electrons do not radiate in those allowed orbits.

2.

Angular Momentum Quantization.
does not follow from stable orbits, energy postulates, or photon emission rules. One must explicitly declare that orbital angular momentum comes in multiples of
h/(2\pi)
.

3.

Energy Level Postulate.
is not derivable from stable orbits, angular-momentum quantization, or transitions alone. Assigning a particular
E_{n}\propto 1/n^{2}
is a separate assumption tied to specific electrostatic coupling and boundary conditions in the hydrogenic system.

4.

Spectral Transition Condition.
does not follow from stable orbits, angular-momentum quantization, or the specific energy formula alone. One must posit that electrons emit/absorb single photons with energies equal to differences of orbit energies.

P.3

Transformations

P.3.1

Axiom 1

Goal:
Transform a subset of Classical Chemistry axioms

S_{0}\;=\;\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\},

into the first Bohr atomic postulate

B_{1}:\;\text{(Stable Orbits)}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{an electron in an allowed orbit does not radiate energy.}

Symbolically, we demonstrate how the idea of discrete atomic units (from
C_{1}
,
C_{3}
,
C_{4}
,
C_{5}
) leads to a model in which electrons remain bound in specific orbits without emitting electromagnetic radiation.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Chemistry, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}}

where

C_{1}:\text{(Atomic Theory)},\hskip 9.24994ptC_{3}:\text{(Definite Composition)},\hskip 9.24994ptC_{4}:\text{(Multiple Proportions)},\hskip 9.24994pt

C_{5}:\text{(Avogadro's Law)}.

Together, these indicate that matter is composed of atoms (each element having characteristic mass), which combine in fixed integer ratios and form countable particles in gases.

Rewrite Step 1: From discrete atoms to internal structure (electrons + nucleus).

\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}\;\longrightarrow\;A_{1},

where

A_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{(Refined Atomic Hypothesis)}\colon\text{each atom contains a positive nucleus}

and negative electrons.

Experiments (e.g. cathode rays, Rutherford scattering) and the discrete mass laws suggest atoms have substructure with distinct charges.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\,A_{1}\}}

A_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Atoms are not indivisible; electrons orbit around a central nucleus (pre-Bohr concept).}

Rewrite Step 2: Planetary model of electron orbits.

A_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;A_{1}^{\prime},

where

A_{1}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{(Planetary Atom)}:\;\text{electrons revolve around a small, dense nucleus}

in classical-like orbits.

Symbolically, we use a “solar system” analogy (Rutherford–Bohr precursor) for the atomic model.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,A_{1}^{\prime}\}}

A_{1}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Classical orbits assumed, no immediate explanation for stability yet.}

Rewrite Step 3: Impose the stability condition (Bohr).

A_{1}^{\prime}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{1},

where

B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{an electron in an allowed orbit does not radiate electromagnetic energy.}

Although classical electrodynamics would predict a radiating, spiraling electron, Bohr postulates certain “allowed” orbits remain stable.

Final Set (Bohr Axiom 1: Stable Orbits):

\boxed{S_{3}=\{\,B_{1}\}}

Hence, from the discrete nature of atoms and their internal substructure, we conclude that electrons can occupy non-radiating orbits around the nucleus.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,A_{1}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,A_{1}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{1}\}}_{S_{3}}.

P.3.2

Axiom 2

Goal:
Transform a subset of Classical Chemistry axioms

S_{0}\;=\;\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\},

into Bohr’s second atomic postulate:

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994ptL_{n}=n\,\frac{h}{2\pi},\hskip 9.24994ptn\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

Symbolically, we illustrate how integer-based combining laws (definite/multiple proportions) and quantized energy insights lead to the statement that the electron’s orbital angular momentum comes only in multiples of
h/(2\pi)
.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Chemistry, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}}

where

C_{1}:\text{(Atomic Theory)},\hskip 9.24994ptC_{3}:\text{(Definite Composition)},\hskip 9.24994ptC_{4}:\text{(Multiple Proportions)},\hskip 9.24994pt

C_{5}:\text{(Avogadro's Law)}.

These collectively imply atoms exist, combine in small integer ratios, and can be enumerated in gases.

Rewrite Step 1: Obtain stable orbits (from previous derivation).

\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{1}.

We already have Bohr’s stable orbits postulate (
B_{1}
), stating electrons do not radiate if in an “allowed orbit.”

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\,B_{1}\}}

B_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{no continuous energy loss for electrons on allowed orbits}.

Rewrite Step 2: Introduce Planck’s constant from discrete spectral lines.

B_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;A_{2},

where

A_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{(Energy Quanta)}:\;E=n\,h\,\nu,\hskip 9.24994ptn\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

Experiments on blackbody radiation and photoelectric effect show energy is quantized, suggesting atomic transitions occur in multiples of
h\,\nu
.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,A_{2}\}}

A_{2}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{quantized energy increments imply discrete allowed transitions}.

Rewrite Step 3: Enforce quantized angular momentum.

A_{2}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{2},

where

B_{2}:\hskip 9.24994ptL_{n}=n\,\frac{h}{2\pi}.

Linking stable orbits to discrete energy steps, Bohr proposed the electron’s orbital angular momentum must be an integer multiple of
\frac{h}{2\pi}
.

Final Set (Bohr Axiom 2: Angular Momentum Quantization):

\boxed{S_{3}=\{\,B_{2}\}}

Thus, the integral ratios observed in chemical combinations parallel the idea of integer multiples in orbital angular momentum.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{1}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,A_{2}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{2}\}}_{S_{3}}.

P.3.3

Axiom 3

Goal:
Transform a subset of Classical Chemistry axioms

S_{0}\;=\;\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\},

into Bohr’s third atomic postulate:

B_{3}:\hskip 9.24994ptE_{n}=-\,\frac{Z^{2}\,e^{2}}{8\pi\epsilon_{0}\,a_{0}}\,\frac{1}{n^{2}},\hskip 9.24994ptn\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

Symbolically, we show how the Coulomb attraction plus angular momentum quantization yields discrete energy levels
\propto 1/n^{2}
.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Chemistry, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}}

Again, these imply the existence of atoms, integral mass ratios, and discrete particles in gases.

Rewrite Step 1: Combine stable orbits
(B_{1})
and angular momentum quantization
(B_{2})
.

\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}\;\longrightarrow\;A_{12},

where

A_{12}:\hskip 9.24994pt(B_{1}+B_{2}):\text{electrons move in stable orbits with }L_{n}=n\frac{h}{2\pi}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\,A_{12}\}}

A_{12}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{foundation of Bohr's first two axioms combined}.

Rewrite Step 2: Apply Coulomb’s law for hydrogen-like atoms.

A_{12}\;\longrightarrow\;A_{12}^{\prime},

where

A_{12}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994ptV(r)=-\,\frac{Ze^{2}}{4\pi\epsilon_{0}}\,\frac{1}{r}.

We identify the electrostatic potential for an electron–nucleus system of charge
Ze
.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,A_{12}^{\prime}\}}

A_{12}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Bohr\textquoteright s quantization + classical Coulomb attraction}.

Rewrite Step 3: Derive discrete energy formula.

A_{12}^{\prime}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{3},

where

B_{3}:\hskip 9.24994ptE_{n}=-\,\frac{Z^{2}\,e^{2}}{8\pi\epsilon_{0}\,a_{0}}\,\frac{1}{n^{2}}.

Balancing centripetal force with electrostatic attraction, plus
L_{n}=n\frac{h}{2\pi}
, yields
r_{n}\propto n^{2}
and
E_{n}\propto 1/n^{2}
.

Final Set (Bohr Axiom 3: Energy Levels):

\boxed{S_{3}=\{\,B_{3}\}}

Hence, each allowed orbit has a specific energy
\propto 1/n^{2}
, explaining the discrete spectral lines for hydrogenic atoms.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,A_{12}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,A_{12}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{3}\}}_{S_{3}}.

P.3.4

Axiom 4

Goal:
Transform a subset of Classical Chemistry axioms

S_{0}\;=\;\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\},

into Bohr’s fourth atomic postulate:

B_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta E=E_{m}-E_{n}=h\,\nu.

Symbolically, we connect discrete orbits (
E_{n}
) to the emission or absorption of photons with energy
h\,\nu
.

Initial Set of Axioms (Classical Chemistry, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}}

The discrete-atom viewpoint plus integral proportion laws underlie countable states.

Rewrite Step 1: Combine earlier Bohr postulates (
B_{1},\,B_{2},\,B_{3}
).

\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}\;\longrightarrow\;A_{123},

where

A_{123}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{electrons in stable orbits with }L_{n}=n\frac{h}{2\pi},\text{ energy }E_{n}\propto\frac{1}{n^{2}}.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{1}=\{\,A_{123}\}}

A_{123}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{a fully specified discrete-orbit model.}

Rewrite Step 2: Experimental observation of discrete spectral lines.

A_{123}\;\longrightarrow\;A_{123}^{\prime},

where

A_{123}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{(Spectroscopy): lines at frequencies }\nu\Rightarrow\text{energies differ by }h\nu.

Empirical data (Balmer, Lyman series) show that atoms absorb/emit only specific frequencies.

Current Set:

\boxed{S_{2}=\{\,A_{123}^{\prime}\}}

A_{123}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{discrete frequency lines reflect transitions between quantized orbits.}

Rewrite Step 3: Conclude
\Delta E=E_{m}-E_{n}=h\,\nu
.

A_{123}^{\prime}\;\longrightarrow\;B_{4},

where

B_{4}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{an electron transitioning between orbit }m\text{ and }n\text{ emits/absorbs photon with }

\Delta E=E_{m}-E_{n}=h\nu.

Thus, each spectral line corresponds to a difference of allowed energy levels.

Final Set (Bohr Axiom 4: Spectral Transitions):

\boxed{S_{3}=\{\,B_{4}\}}

Hence, the discrete energies
E_{n}
produce quantized photon frequencies upon electronic transitions, matching observed spectra.

Symbolic Evolution (Compact Form)
:

\underbrace{\{\,C_{1},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5}\}}_{S_{0}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,A_{123}\}}_{S_{1}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,A_{123}^{\prime}\}}_{S_{2}}~\longrightarrow~\underbrace{\{\,B_{4}\}}_{S_{3}}.

Overall
, these transformations illustrate how classical chemistry’s fundamental ideas (existence of atoms, definite and multiple proportions, and particle count in gases) can be combined with additional physical postulates to obtain Bohr’s four axioms for atomic structure.

Appendix Q

From Geocentric to Heliocentric Model

Q.1

Geocentric Model

The geocentric model (often attributed to Aristotle and Ptolemy) places Earth at the center of all celestial motions.
Every other celestial body—Sun, Moon, planets, stars—revolves around Earth in circles or sums of circles (epicycles).
Below is a minimal and complete set of axioms, each of which is independent (i.e. it cannot be derived from the others) and collectively sufficient to account for planetary motions, retrograde loops, and stellar rotation around Earth.

Q.1.1

Axioms

1.

Central Earth

\forall\,X\neq\mathrm{Earth}:\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{CenterOrbit}(X)=\mathrm{Earth}.

All celestial bodies
X
have their primary orbital centers at Earth (which is fixed and unmoving).

2.

Uniform Circular Motion

\forall\,X:\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{Orbit}(X)=\sum(\text{UniformCircles}),

i.e., each orbit is either a single circle or a finite sum of epicyclic circles, all traversed with constant angular velocity.

3.

Sphere of Fixed Stars

\exists\,S_{\star}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{all stars are on }S_{\star})\;\wedge\;(\mathrm{dist}(S_{\star},\mathrm{Earth})=\text{constant}),

where
S_{\star}
is a single spherical shell (or near-sphere) centered on Earth, rotating daily to carry the stars around.

4.

Epicycles for Planets

\forall\,P\in\{\text{planets}\}:\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{Orbit}(P)=(\text{deferent circle})+(\text{epicycle circle}),

where each planet
P
moves on a small circle (epicycle) whose center itself moves along a larger circle (deferent) around Earth, explaining retrograde motion and varying brightness.

Q.1.2

Completeness

1.

Central Earth.
(Axiom 1) fixes Earth as the motionless center for all celestial bodies.

2.

Uniform Circular Motion & Fixed-Star Sphere.
Axioms 2 and 3 assert that celestial paths are composed of one or more uniform circles and that stars reside on a single rotating sphere, accounting for daily rotation and consistent stellar distances.

3.

Epicycles.
(Axiom 4) extends the simple circular notion to capture observed planetary anomalies (retrograde, brightness changes) by introducing a small circle (epicycle) superimposed on a main orbit (deferent).

From these four, the classical Ptolemaic geocentric cosmology can be constructed, producing qualitative and quantitative predictions for planetary positions, albeit requiring increasingly intricate epicycles for higher precision.

Q.1.3

Independence

1.

Central Earth.
cannot be deduced from uniform circular motion, the star sphere, or epicyclic constructs. One must explicitly posit that orbits are centered on Earth.

2.

Uniform Circular Motion.
is not derivable from Earth-centrism, the star sphere, or epicycles alone; it establishes a geometric ideal for celestial movements (constant angular velocities on perfect circles).

3.

Sphere of Fixed Stars.
does not follow from Earth-centrism, uniform circles, or planetary epicycles. It specifically asserts that all stars are affixed to a single spherical shell around Earth.

4.

Epicycles.
cannot be inferred from the other three axioms. Even with Earth-centrism and uniform circles for some bodies plus the star sphere, explicit epicycles are required to handle planetary retrograde motions and brightness variations.

Q.2

Heliocentric Model

The heliocentric model (famously articulated by Copernicus) places the Sun at (or near) the center of planetary orbits.
Earth is treated as one among several planets revolving around the Sun, while its daily rotation on its own axis accounts for the apparent motions of the Sun and stars.
Below is a minimal and complete set of axioms, each of which is independent (i.e., it cannot be derived from the others) and collectively sufficient to describe planetary motions, retrograde loops, and the lack of observable stellar parallax (under the assumption of extremely distant stars).

Q.2.1

Axioms

1.

Central Sun

\forall\,P\neq\text{Sun}:\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{CenterOrbit}(P)=\text{Sun}.

All planets
P
(including Earth) move around the Sun as the primary orbital center.

2.

Earth’s Dual Motion

\mathrm{Rotation}(\mathrm{Earth},24\text{h})\;\wedge\;\mathrm{Revolution}(\mathrm{Earth},T_{\oplus})

Earth both rotates on its own axis once per day (producing daily cycles) and orbits the Sun with period
T_{\oplus}\!\approx\!1\text{ year}
.

3.

Uniform Circular Orbits (Copernican Ideal)

\forall\,(\mathrm{planet}\ P):\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{Orbit}(P)=\sum(\text{UniformCircles}),

each planet’s path around the Sun is modeled as one or more perfect circles (possibly epicycles) traversed with constant angular speed, accounting for observed phenomena such as retrograde motion.

4.

Distant Fixed Stars & Minimal Parallax

\exists\,S_{\star}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\mathrm{dist}(S_{\star},\text{Sun})\gg\mathrm{dist}(\text{planet},\text{Sun}))\;\wedge\;(\text{stellar parallax}\approx 0).

Stars lie on a vastly larger sphere (or shell) far beyond planetary distances, so any annual stellar parallax is negligible (explaining why no parallax was detected with then-current instruments).

Q.2.2

Completeness

1.

Central Sun.
(Axiom 1) places the Sun at the orbital focus for all planets, including Earth.

2.

Earth’s Dual Motion.
(Axiom 2) explains daily cycles (Earth’s rotation) and seasonal phenomena (Earth’s annual revolution).

3.

Uniform Circular Orbits.
(Axiom 3) maintains the classical ideal of circular (or epicyclic) paths at uniform angular velocities, reproducing retrograde loops from the new perspective of Earth’s own motion.

4.

Distant Stars.
(Axiom 4) accounts for the absence of observed stellar parallax, attributing it to the immense distance of the fixed stars relative to planetary orbits.

From these four axioms, one reconstructs a Copernican-style heliocentric cosmology, explaining the same phenomena as geocentrism but with Earth treated as a moving planet, simplifying the overall structure of planetary orbits.

Q.2.3

Independence

1.

Central Sun.
cannot be derived from Earth’s dual motion, uniform circular orbits, or distant stars alone. It must be stated that the Sun (not Earth) is the center of planetary paths.

2.

Earth’s Dual Motion.
is not inferable from the Sun-centric arrangement, purely circular orbits, or the distant star sphere. One must explicitly claim that Earth rotates once per day and revolves annually.

3.

Uniform Circular Orbits.
does not follow from any combination of a Sun-centered system, Earth’s rotation/revolution, or distant stars. It specifically asserts the classical geometric principle that planetary paths are formed by perfect circles (or sums thereof).

4.

Distant Fixed Stars.
is not implied by the other three axioms. One must introduce the idea that the stars are so far away that no measurable parallax is observed, reconciling the lack of star shift throughout Earth’s orbit.

Q.3

Transformations

Q.3.1

Axiom 1

Goal:
Transform the
Geocentric Axiom 1

G_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,X\neq\mathrm{Earth}:\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{CenterOrbit}(X)=\mathrm{Earth},

into the
Heliocentric Axiom 1

H_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\,P\neq\text{Sun}:\hskip 9.24994pt\mathrm{CenterOrbit}(P)=\text{Sun}.

Symbolically, we show how the premise “All orbits are centered on Earth” evolves into “All planetary orbits are centered on the Sun,” with Earth itself counted among the orbiting planets.

Initial Axiom (Geocentric, Subset):

\boxed{S_{0}=\bigl\{G_{1}\bigr\}}

where

G_{1}:\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Central Earth}:\ \forall\,X\neq\mathrm{Earth}:\ \mathrm{CenterOrbit}(X)=\mathrm{Earth}.

This states that all celestial objects revolve about Earth as their orbital center.

Rewrite Step 1: Recognize Observational Challenges (Retrograde & Epicycle Complexity).

G_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;G_{1}^{\prime},

where

G_{1}^{\prime}:\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{epicycles grow in complexity to explain planetary retrograde loops and brightness changes}).
